2g-1+|\mathcal{A}|+n
, so one has an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\cong(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))^{2g-2+|\mathcal{A}|+n}
.

Figure 7.
The geometric representatives of a set of generators for
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a connected marked surface with at least two boundary arcs. Then
X(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is a smooth variety.

For
g=\begin{pmatrix}g_{++}&g_{+-}\\
g_{-+}&g_{--}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}
, the Zariski tangent space
T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is:

T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}=\left\{X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{++}&X_{+-}\\
X_{-+}&X_{--}\end{pmatrix},\mbox{ s.t. }\det(g+\varepsilon X)\equiv 1\pmod{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\\
=\left\{X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{++}&X_{+-}\\
X_{-+}&X_{--}\end{pmatrix},\mbox{ s.t. }g_{--}X_{++}+g_{++}X_{--}=g_{-+}X_{+-}+g_{+-}X_{-+}\right\}.

Similarly, if
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
(i.e. if
g_{++}=0
) then

T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}=\left\{X=\begin{pmatrix}0&X_{+-}\\
X_{-+}&X_{--}\end{pmatrix},\mbox{ s.t. }g_{+-}X_{-+}+g_{-+}X_{+-}=0\right\}.

Lemma 3.6
.

(1)

Let
X=\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and consider the regular map
F:X\to\mathbb{C}
,
F(h,g)=(hg)_{--}
. Let
x=(h,g)\in X
. Then there exists
v\in T_{h}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\subset T_{h}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\oplus T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}=T_{x}X
such that
D_{x}F(v)\neq 0
.

(2)

Let
Y:=\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\times(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{2}
and consider the regular map
F:Y\to\mathbb{C}
,
F(h,g,f):=(ghg^{-1}f)_{--}
. Let
y=(h,g,f)\in Y
. Then there exists
v\in T_{h}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\oplus T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\subset T_{h}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\oplus T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\oplus T_{f}\operatorname{SL}_{2}=T_{y}Y
such that
D_{y}F(v)\neq 0
.

Proof.

(1)
Let
X_{1}^{h},X_{2}^{h}\in T_{h}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\subset T_{x}X
be the vectors
X_{1}^{h}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
,
X_{2}^{h}=\begin{pmatrix}0&h_{+-}\\
-h_{-+}&0\end{pmatrix}
. Then
D_{x}F(X_{1}^{h})=g_{--}
and
D_{x}F(X_{2}^{h})=-g_{+-}
. Since
\det(g)=1
, either
g_{--}\neq 0
or
g_{+-}\neq 0
.

(2)
Consider the vectors
X_{1}^{h},X_{2}^{h},X^{g}\in T_{y}Y=T_{h}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\oplus T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\oplus T_{f}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
defined by
X_{1}^{h}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in T_{h}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\subset T_{y}Y
,
X_{2}^{h}=\begin{pmatrix}0&h_{+-}\\
-h_{-+}&0\end{pmatrix}\in T_{h}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\subset T_{y}Y
and
X^{g}=\begin{pmatrix}0&g_{+-}\\
-g_{-+}&0\end{pmatrix}\in T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\subset T_{y}Y
.
One has
D_{y}F(X_{1}^{h})=g_{--}(g^{-1}f)_{--}
and
D_{y}F(X_{2}^{h})=h_{+-}g_{-+}(g^{-1}f)_{--}-h_{-+}g_{--}(g^{-1}f)_{+-}
so
D_{y}F(X_{1}^{h})=D_{y}F(X_{2}^{h})=0
if and only if
g_{--}=(g^{-1}f)_{--}=0
. In this case, one finds that

D_{y}F(X^{g})=-g_{+-}(gh)_{-+}f_{--}-g_{-+}(hg^{-1}f)_{+-}+g_{-+}(gh)_{--}f_{--}=(g_{-+})^{2}h_{+-}f_{--}.

Since
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}
,
g_{+-}g_{-+}=-1
and
g_{-+}\neq 0
. Similarly,
h_{+-}\neq 0
. If
f_{--}=0
then
f_{+-}\neq 0
and the equality
(g^{-1}f)_{--}=0
would imply
g_{-+}f_{+-}=0
which is a contradiction. Therefore
f_{--}\neq 0
and
D_{y}F(X^{g})\neq 0
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Let
g
be the genus of
\Sigma
and
n
the number of its boundary components. Let
\mathbb{G}
be the generating set of Example
3.4
. Let us first suppose that
\mathring{\mathcal{P}}\neq\emptyset
. In this case, we can remove from
\mathbb{G}
one generator
\gamma_{p}
to obtain a presenting graph
\Gamma
such that every corner arc is an edge of
\Gamma
. We obtain an isomorphism

X(\mathbf{\Sigma})\xrightarrow[\cong]{\Psi_{w}^{*}}\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\xrightarrow[\cong]{\varphi_{\Gamma}}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))^{2g-2+n}\times(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1})^{|\mathcal{A}|}.

So
X(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is smooth and irreducible because both
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}
are smooth and irreducible.
If
\mathring{\mathcal{P}}=\emptyset
, let
p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}
, let
\Gamma
be the presenting graph obtained from
\mathbb{G}
by removing
\alpha_{p_{0}}
and consider the variety

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}:=\{\rho:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2},\mbox{ such that }\rho(\alpha(p))\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\mbox{ for all }p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}\setminus\{p_{0}\}\}.

The morphism
\varphi_{\Gamma}
restricts to an isomorphism
\varphi_{\Gamma}:\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cong(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))^{2g+n-1}\times(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1})^{|\mathcal{A}|-1}
, so
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a smooth irreducible variety.
Let
F:\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{C}
be the regular function
F=X_{++}^{\alpha(p_{0})}
, i.e.
F(\rho)=\rho(\alpha(p_{0}))_{++}
. One has
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=F^{-1}(0)
so it suffices to prove that for every
\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, one has
D_{\rho}F\neq 0
. Using
\varphi_{\Gamma}
we identify the Zariski tangent space
T_{\rho}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
with

T_{\rho}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cong\oplus_{p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}\setminus\{p_{0}\}}T_{\rho(\alpha(p))}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\oplus\oplus_{\beta\in\mathbb{G}\setminus\{\alpha(p)\}}T_{\rho(\beta)}\operatorname{SL}_{2}.

First suppose that
\mathbf{\Sigma}
contains two boundary edges in the same boundary component
\partial
. We suppose that
p_{0}\in\partial
so one has two punctures
p_{0},p_{1}\in\partial
consecutive in the cyclic order of
\partial
.
By definition of
\mathbb{G}
, there exists a path
\beta
which is a composition of the elements of
\mathbb{G}\setminus\{\alpha(p_{0}),\alpha(p_{1})\}
such that
\alpha(p_{0})\alpha(p_{1})\beta=1_{s(\alpha(p_{0}))}
. Therefore

F(\rho)=\rho(\alpha(p_{0}))_{++}=\rho(\alpha(p_{0})^{-1})_{--}=\left(\rho(\alpha(p_{1}))\rho(\beta)\right)_{--}.

By the first item of Lemma
3.6
, for every
\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, there exists
v\in T_{\rho(\alpha(p_{1}))}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\subset T_{\rho}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
such that
D_{\rho}F(v)\neq 0
.

If
\mathbf{\Sigma}
only contains boundary components having a single boundary arc, one can find a boundary component
\partial
whose unique puncture
p_{1}\in\partial
is distinct from
p_{0}
. By definition of
\mathbb{G}
, there exists a path
\beta
which is a composition of the elements of
\mathbb{G}\setminus\{\alpha(p_{0}),\alpha(p_{1}),\delta_{\partial}\}
such that
\alpha(p_{0})^{-1}=\delta_{\partial}\alpha(p_{1})\delta_{\partial}^{-1}\beta
, so

F(\rho)=\left(\rho(\delta_{\partial})\rho(\alpha(p_{1}))\rho(\delta_{\partial})^{-1}\rho(\beta)\right)_{--}.

By the second item of Lemma
3.6
, for every
\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, there exists
v\in T_{\rho(\alpha(p_{1}))}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\oplus T_{\rho(\delta_{\partial})}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\subset T_{\rho}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
such that
D_{\rho}F(v)\neq 0
.
In every cases, we have thus proved that
D_{\rho}F\neq 0
for every
\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, so
X(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is smooth.
∎

Definition 3.7

(Central at inner punctures)
.

Let
x\in X(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and write
\rho_{x}:=\overline{\Psi}_{w}^{-1}(x)\in\overline{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. For
p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}
an inner puncture, we say that
x

is central at

p
if
\rho_{x}(\gamma_{p})=\pm id
.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be an essential marked surface with at least two boundary arcs. Then the regular locus of
\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the subset of elements
\widehat{x}=(x,h_{p},h_{\partial})
such that for every
p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}
then either
x
is not central at
p
or
h_{p}=\pm 2
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Consider the application
f:\operatorname{SL}_{2}\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
,
f:(g,z)\mapsto\operatorname{tr}(g)+T_{N}(z)
and set
\widehat{X}:=f^{-1}(0)
. Then
(g,z)\in\widehat{X}
is smooth if and only if either
g\neq\pm\mathds{1}
or
g=\pm\mathds{1}
and

z=\mp 2
.

Proof.

First, note that for
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
z^{1/N}
an
N
-th root of
z
, the equation
T_{N}(X)=z+z^{-1}
has solutions
X=z^{n/N}+z^{-n/N}
for
n\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
counted with multiplicity, i.e.
T_{N}(X)-(z+z^{-1})=\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}(X-z^{n/N}-z^{-n/N})
. Therefore, for
c\in\mathbb{C}
, the polynomial
P_{N,c}(X):=T_{N}(X)-c
has roots of multiplicity
\geq 2
if and only if
c=\pm 2
in which case the multiple roots are
X=\pm(q^{n}+q^{-n})
with
n\in\{1,\ldots,(N-3)/2\}
.

For
g=\begin{pmatrix}g_{++}&g_{+-}\\
g_{-+}&g_{--}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}
, let us consider the tangent space

T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}=\left\{X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{++}&X_{+-}\\
X_{-+}&X_{--}\end{pmatrix},\mbox{ s.t. }g_{--}X_{++}+g_{++}X_{--}=g_{-+}X_{+-}+g_{+-}X_{-+}\right\}

as before. Then the differential at
(g,z)\in\widehat{X}
is computed as

D_{(g,z)}f:T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C},\quad D_{(g,z)}f:(X,z_{0})\mapsto\operatorname{tr}(X)+T_{N}^{\prime}(z)z_{0}.

In order to find at which point
f
is a submersion, we need to find when this differential vanishes. This will happen if and only if we have the two conditions:
(i)

\operatorname{tr}(X)=0
for all
X\in T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
(ii)

T_{N}^{\prime}(z)=0
. By the preceding discussion,
T_{N}^{\prime}(z)=0
if and only if
\operatorname{tr}(g)=\pm 2
and
z\neq\mp 2
. Let us analyse
(i)
.
First suppose that
g_{+-}\neq 0
. Then for every
x\in\mathbb{C}
we have
X:=\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
x\frac{g_{--}}{g_{+-}}&0\end{pmatrix}\in T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
\operatorname{tr}(X)=x
so
(i)
is not satisfied. Similarly, if
g_{-+}\neq 0
, we can use
X:=\begin{pmatrix}x&x\frac{g_{--}}{g_{-+}}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\in T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
\operatorname{tr}(X)=x
so
(i)
is not satisfied. If
g=\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
0&x^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
then every
X\in T_{g}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
satisfies
\operatorname{tr}(X)=(1-x^{-2})X_{++}
so
(i)
is satisfied if and only if
g=\pm\mathds{1}
in which case
(ii)
is satisfied if and only if
z=\mp(q^{n}+q^{-n})
for
n\in\{1,\ldots,(N-3)/2\}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Write
\mathring{\mathcal{P}}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{s}\}
and consider the composition

g:\overline{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\times\mathbb{C}^{s}\xrightarrow{h}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}\times\mathbb{C})^{s}\xrightarrow{(f,\ldots,f)}\mathbb{C}^{s}

where
g(\rho,\{h_{p_{i}}\}_{i}):=(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}),h_{p_{i}})_{p_{i}}
and
f
is defined in Lemma
3.9
. Let
X^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma}):=g^{-1}(0,\ldots,0)
and consider the covering
\pi:\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to X^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
defined by
\pi(x,h_{p_{i}},h_{\partial}):=(\rho_{x},h_{p_{i}})
which consists in forgetting the boundary invariants
h_{\partial}
. For
(\rho_{x},h_{p})\in X^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
, set
c_{\partial}:=\rho_{x}(\alpha_{\partial})
. The fiber
\pi^{-1}(\rho_{x},h_{p_{i}})
consists in the set of elements
(x,h_{p_{i}},h_{\partial})
where
the
h_{\partial}
satisfy
h_{\partial}^{N}=c_{\partial}
so are zeroes of the polynomials
X^{N}-c_{\partial}
. Since the polynomials
X^{N}-c_{\partial}
have
N
zeroes each with multiplicity one,
\pi
is a regular covering so the smooth locus of
\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the pull-back by
\pi
of the smooth locus of
X^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Since
h
is a submersion on
X^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
, by the chain rule
g
is a submersion at
(\rho,\{h_{p_{i}}\}_{i})
if and only if for every
i

f
is a submersion at
(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}),h_{p_{i}})
. By Lemma
3.9
, this condition is equivalent to the condition that for every
p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}
, either
\rho(\gamma_{p})\neq\pm\mathds{1}
or
h_{p}=\mp 2
. This concludes the proof.

∎

3.3.
Behavior for the gluing operation

The goal of this subsection is to prove the

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}
be connected marked surfaces and for
i=1,2
let
a_{i}
be a boundary edges of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i}
such that the connected component of
\partial\Sigma_{i}
which contains
a_{i}
also contains at least another boundary edge.
Then the maps
p_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}:{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})\to{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}\cup_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
and
\widehat{p}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}:\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})\to\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}\cup_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
are surjective.

To prove Theorem
3.10
, it is useful to introduce an alternative geometric interpretation of
X(\mathbf{\Sigma})
defined in
[
CL22
]
. Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a connected essential surface and fix a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
. Let
\pi:U\Sigma\to\Sigma
be the unitary tangent bundle, so a point
\overrightarrow{v}\in U\Sigma
is given by a pair
\overrightarrow{v}=(p,v)
where
p\in\Sigma
and
v\in T_{p}\Sigma
has norm one.
For such a
\overrightarrow{v}=(p,v)
, write
-\overrightarrow{v}:=(p,-v)
.

Recall that the orientation of
\Sigma
induces an orientation of
\partial\Sigma
.
For
p\in\partial\Sigma
, let
\overrightarrow{p}\in U\Sigma
be
\overrightarrow{p}:=(p,v)
where
v\in T_{p}\partial\Sigma\subset T_{p}\Sigma
is the unitary tangent vector oriented in the direction of
\partial\Sigma
. Said differently, if
n\in T_{p}\Sigma
is a vector normal to
\partial\Sigma
which points outside of
\Sigma
, then
(n,v)
is a positive basis of
T_{p}\Sigma
. Write
\widehat{\mathbb{V}}:=\{\pm\overrightarrow{v_{a}},a\in\mathcal{A}\}
(recall that
v_{a}
is a point in the middle of the boundary edge
a
).

For
\overrightarrow{v}\in U\Sigma
with
p=\pi(\overrightarrow{v})
, let
\theta_{\overrightarrow{v}}\in\pi_{1}(U\Sigma,\overrightarrow{v})
be the homotopy class of the path in
U\Sigma
which lies inside the circle fiber
\pi^{-1}(p)=T_{p}\Sigma
and makes a single positive turn. Also denote by
\theta_{\overrightarrow{v}}^{1/2}
be the homotopy class of the path starting at
\overrightarrow{v}
and ending at
-\overrightarrow{v}
which makes a positive half turn in the fiber
T_{p}\Sigma
.

Let
\alpha
be an oriented arc of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
with endpoints in some boundary edges
a
and
b
such that
\alpha
is oriented from
a
to
b
. Let
c_{\alpha}:[0,1]\to\Sigma
be an oriented embedding such that
(1)

c_{\alpha}([0,1])
is isotopic to
\alpha
with the same orientation,
(2)

c_{\alpha}^{\prime}(0)=-\overrightarrow{v_{a}}
and
c_{\alpha}^{\prime}(1)=\overrightarrow{v_{b}}
and
(3)

c_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)
has norm
1
for all
t\in[0,1]
. We denote by
\widehat{\alpha}
the homotopy class of the path
[0,1]\to U\Sigma
,
t\mapsto(c_{\alpha}(t),c^{\prime}_{\alpha}(t))
.

Definition 3.11

(Twisted relative representation varieties)
.

(1)

The
twisted relative representation variety

\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the set of functors
\widehat{\rho}:\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma,\widehat{\mathbb{V}})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
such that
\widehat{\rho}(\theta^{1/2}_{\overrightarrow{v}})=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
for all
\overrightarrow{v}\in\widehat{\mathbb{V}}
. It admits a natural structure of affine variety as in Definition
3.1

(2)

The
reduced twisted relative representation variety

\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the subvariety of
\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
of functors
\widehat{\rho}
such that
\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha(p)})
is upper triangular for all
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}
.

Theorem 3.12
.

(
[
CL22
, Theorem
8.12
]
) There exists an isomorphism
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}:X(\mathbf{\Sigma})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
sending a character
\chi:\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathbb{C}
to the functor
\widehat{\rho}:\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma,\widehat{\mathbb{V}})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
characterized by the fact that for every arc
\alpha
, then

\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha})=\begin{pmatrix}\chi(\alpha_{++})&\chi(\alpha_{+-})\\
\chi(\alpha_{-+})&\chi(\alpha_{--})\end{pmatrix}.

In particular, the ring isomorphism
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{*}
sends a bad arc
\alpha(p)_{-+}
to the function
X_{-+}^{\widehat{\alpha(p)}}
which sends
\widehat{\rho}
to the lower left matrix coefficient of
\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha(p)})
, so we get the

Corollary 3.13
.

The isomorphism
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
restricts to an isomorphism (still denoted by the same letter)
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}:\overline{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\xrightarrow{\cong}\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Now consider
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}
with
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
as in Theorem
3.10
and write
\mathbf{\Sigma}:=\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}\cup_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}
.
For
i=1,2
, let
\iota_{i}:\Sigma_{i}\hookrightarrow\Sigma
be the inclusion map.
Fix a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
which induces, by restriction, some Riemannian metrics on
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
. Let
\overrightarrow{w}\in U\Sigma
be the image of
\overrightarrow{v_{a_{1}}}\in U\Sigma_{1}\xrightarrow{\subset}U\Sigma
by
(\iota_{1})_{*}
. Note that the image of
\overrightarrow{v_{a_{2}}}\in U\Sigma_{2}
in
U\Sigma
by
(\iota_{2})_{*}
is
-\overrightarrow{v}
.
Write
\Pi_{1}:=\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma_{1},\mathbb{V}_{1})
,
\Pi_{2}:=\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma_{2},\mathbb{V}_{2})
and
\Pi:=\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma,\mathbb{V}\cup\{\overrightarrow{w}\})
and consider the variety:

\mathcal{R}^{w}:=\{\widehat{\rho}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Pi,\operatorname{SL}_{2}),\widehat{\rho}(\theta^{1/2}_{\overrightarrow{v}})=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\mbox{, for all }\overrightarrow{v}\in\widehat{\mathbb{V}}\}.

For
i=1,2
, the embedding
\iota_{i}:\Sigma_{i}\hookrightarrow\Sigma
induces a fully faithful functor
(\iota_{i})_{*}:\Pi_{i}\hookrightarrow\Pi
so we can consider
\Pi_{i}
as a full subcategory of
\Pi
. We are in the following situation:

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
G
be a groupoid and
G_{1},G_{2}\subset G
be two full subcategories of
G
and
v\in G
an object such that

(1)

one has
\operatorname{Ob}(G_{1})\cup\operatorname{Ob}(G_{2})=\operatorname{Ob}(G)
and
\operatorname{Ob}(G_{1})\cap\operatorname{Ob}(G_{2})=\{v\}
;

(2)

for
v_{1}\in G_{1}
and
v_{2}\in G_{2}
, the composition map
G_{1}(v_{1},v)\times G_{2}(v,v_{2})\to G(v_{1},v_{2})
is a bijection.

Then the restriction map
\operatorname{res}:\operatorname{Hom}(G,\operatorname{SL}_{2})\to\operatorname{Hom}(G_{1},\operatorname{SL}_{2})\times\operatorname{Hom}(G_{2},\operatorname{SL}_{2})
sending
\rho
to the pair
(\mathchoice{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,G_{1}}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,G_{1}}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=2.12971pt}}_{\,G_{1}}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=2.71527pt}}_{\,G_{1}}},\mathchoice{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,G_{2}}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,G_{2}}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=2.12971pt}}_{\,G_{2}}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=2.71527pt}}_{\,G_{2}}})
, is a bijection.

Proof.

Consider the map

F:\operatorname{Hom}(G_{1},\operatorname{SL}_{2})\times\operatorname{Hom}(G_{2},\operatorname{SL}_{2})\to\operatorname{Hom}(G,\operatorname{SL}_{2})

which sends a pair
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
to the functor
\rho
such that for
v_{1},v_{2}\in G
, the restriction
\rho:G(v_{1},v_{2})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is equal to
(i)
the restriction
\rho_{1}:G_{1}(v_{1},v_{2})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
if
v_{1},v_{2}\in\Pi_{1}
,
(ii)
to the restriction
\rho_{2}:G_{2}(v_{1},v_{2})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
if
v_{1},v_{2}\in G_{2}
,
(iii)
to the composition
G(v_{1},v_{2})\cong G_{1}(v_{1},v)\times G_{2}(v,v_{2})\xrightarrow{(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\times}\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
if
v_{1}\in G_{1}
,
v_{2}\in G_{2}
,
(iv)
to a similar composition map with
1
and
2
exchanged if
v_{1}\in G_{2}
and
v_{2}\in G_{1}
.
F
is clearly the inverse of
\operatorname{res}
.
∎

The morphism
F
of the proof, obtained by replacing
(G,G_{1},G_{2},v)
by
(\Pi,\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\overrightarrow{w})
, induces by restriction an isomorphism (still denoted by the same letter):

F:\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{R}^{w}.

The inclusion
\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma,\widehat{\mathbb{V}})\subset\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma,\widehat{\mathbb{V}}\cup\{\overrightarrow{w}\})
induces a regular (restriction) map
G:\mathcal{R}^{w}\to\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Lemma 3.15
.

The composition
G\circ F:\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})\to\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is surjective and induces by restriction a surjective map

\varphi:\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

In order to prove Lemma
3.15
, we first prove the following:

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
B^{+}\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be the subgroup of upper triangular matrices and let
A,B,C,D\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be such that
AB\in B^{+}
and
CD\in B^{+}
. Then there exists
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
such that
Ag
,
g^{-1}B
,
Dg^{-1}
and
gC
are in
B^{+}
.

Proof.

Write
A=\begin{pmatrix}a_{++}&a_{+-}\\
a_{-+}&a_{--}\end{pmatrix}
with similar notations for
B,C,D,g
. Then

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Ag\in B^{+}\\
g^{-1}B\in B^{+}\end{array}\right.\Leftrightarrow\begin{pmatrix}a_{-+}&a_{--}\\
b_{-+}&-b_{++}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}g_{++}\\
g_{-+}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Since
AB\in B^{+}\Leftrightarrow \det\begin{pmatrix}a_{-+}&a_{--}\\
b_{-+}&-b_{++}\end{pmatrix}=0
, one can find
(g_{++},g_{-+})\neq(0,0)
which satisfies this equation. Similarly,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Dg^{-1}\in B^{+}\\
gC\in B^{+}\end{array}\right.\Leftrightarrow\begin{pmatrix}c_{++}&c_{-+}\\
-d_{--}&d_{-+}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}g_{-+}\\
g_{--}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Since
CD\in B^{+}\Leftrightarrow\det\begin{pmatrix}c_{++}&c_{-+}\\
-d_{--}&d_{-+}\end{pmatrix}=0
, one can find
(g_{-+},g_{--})\neq(0,0)
which satisfies this equation. Up to multiplying
(g_{-+},g_{--})
by a non zero scalar, one can further suppose that
g_{++}g_{--}-g_{+-}g_{-+}=1
, so the matrix
g=\begin{pmatrix}g_{++}&g_{+-}\\
g_{-+}&g_{--}\end{pmatrix}
satisfies the conclusion of the lemma.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.15
.

Since
F
is a bijection, we need to prove that
G
is surjective. Let us first define an action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on
\mathcal{R}^{w}
as follows. For
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{w}
, we define
g\bullet\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{w}
as the functor which sends a path
\beta:v_{1}\to v_{2}
in
\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma,\widehat{\mathbb{V}}\cup\{\overrightarrow{w}\})
to

(g\bullet\rho)(\beta):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho(\beta)&\mbox{, if }v_{1}\neq\overrightarrow{w},v_{2}\neq\overrightarrow{w};\\
\rho(\beta)g&\mbox{, if }v_{1}\neq\overrightarrow{w},v_{2}=\overrightarrow{w};\\
g^{-1}\rho(\beta)&\mbox{, if }v_{1}=\overrightarrow{w},v_{2}\neq\overrightarrow{w};\\
g^{-1}\rho(\beta)g&\mbox{, if }v_{1}=v_{2}=\overrightarrow{w}.\end{array}\right.

Clearly
G(g\bullet\rho)=G(\rho)
so
G
induces a map
G^{\prime}:{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{R}^{w}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})$}\right.}\to\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. Let us prove that
G^{\prime}
is surjective (it is not difficult to prove that it is an isomorphism).

Let
c
be a boundary edge of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
(which exists by the hypotheses made in Theorem
3.10
) and consider an arbitrary path
\beta:\overrightarrow{v_{c}}\to\overrightarrow{w}
. Let
\rho\in\mathcal{R}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and define an extension
\widehat{\rho}\in\mathcal{R}^{w}
as follows. First, the restriction of
\widehat{\rho}
to
\Pi_{1}(U\Sigma,\widehat{\mathbb{V}})
is
\rho
and
\widehat{\rho}(\beta)=\mathds{1}_{2}
. Next for a path
\gamma:\overrightarrow{w}\to\overrightarrow{v}
with
\overrightarrow{v}\in\widehat{V}
, set
\widehat{\rho}(\gamma):=\rho(\gamma\beta^{-1})
. Similarly for a path
\gamma^{\prime}:\overrightarrow{v}\to\overrightarrow{w}
set
\widehat{\rho}(\gamma^{\prime}):=\rho(\beta^{-1}\gamma^{\prime})
. Clearly
\widehat{\rho}\in\mathcal{R}^{w}
and
G(\widehat{\rho})=\rho
so
G
and
G^{\prime}
are surjective.

Let us prove that the restriction
\varphi
of
G\circ F
is surjective as well. Let
p_{1},p_{1}^{\prime}
be the two punctures adjacent to
a_{1}
and
p_{2},p_{2}^{\prime}
the two punctures adjacent to
a_{2}
such that while gluing
a_{1}
with
a_{2}
,
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
have the same image
p\in\partial\Sigma
and
p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime}
have the same image
p^{\prime}\in\partial\Sigma
(see Figure
9
). By assumption,
p_{1}\neq p_{1}^{\prime}
and
p_{2}\neq p_{2}^{\prime}
.
Let
\rho\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. By surjectivity of
G
, there exists
\widehat{\rho}\in\mathcal{R}^{w}
such that
G(\widehat{\rho})=\rho
. Then
\widehat{\rho}
is the image by
F
of an element of

\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
if and only if for all
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial_{1}}\cup\mathcal{P}^{\partial_{2}}
, then
\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha(p)})\in B^{+}
. Since
G(\widehat{\rho})=\rho\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
,
\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha(p)})\in B^{+}
for all
p\notin\{p_{1},p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2},p_{2}^{\prime}\}
. Write
A:=\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha(p_{1})})
,
B:=\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha(p_{2})})
,
C:=\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha(p_{2}^{\prime})})
,
D:=\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})})
. Then
AB=\rho(\widehat{\alpha(p)})\in B^{+}
and
CD=\rho(\widehat{\alpha(p^{\prime})})\in B^{+}
so by Lemma
3.16
, there exists
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
such that
Ag
,
g^{-1}B
,
Dg^{-1}
and
gC
are in
B^{+}
. Therefore, writing
\rho^{\prime}:=g\bullet\widehat{\rho}
one has
\mathchoice{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.41428pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.01427pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.63649pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.37779pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}}}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})
,
\mathchoice{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.41428pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\Pi_{2}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.01427pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\Pi_{2}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.63649pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\Pi_{2}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.37779pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\Pi_{2}}}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
and
\varphi(\mathchoice{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.41428pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.01427pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.63649pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.37779pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}}},\mathchoice{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.41428pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\Pi_{2})}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.01427pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\Pi_{2})}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.63649pt,depth=2.41304pt}}_{\,\Pi_{2})}}{{\rho^{\prime}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.37779pt,depth=2.99861pt}}_{\,\Pi_{2})}}=\rho
. So
\varphi
is surjective.

∎

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

The surjectivity of
p_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
follows from the commutativity of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times X(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\mathbf{\Sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{tw}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}}\times\Psi_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

together with the surjectivity of
\varphi
proved in Lemma
3.15
. Let us prove that
\widehat{p}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
is surjective as well. Let
\widehat{x}=({x},h_{p},h_{\partial})_{p,\partial}\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and fix
(x_{1},x_{2})\in X(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times X(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
such that
p_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})=x
. Let
\partial_{1}
be the boundary component of
\Sigma_{1}
containing
a_{1}
and
\partial_{2}
the boundary component of
\Sigma_{2}
containing
a_{2}
and
\partial^{\prime}
the boundary component of
\Sigma
obtained by
1
-surgery on
\partial_{1}\cup\partial_{2}
. By definition
h_{\partial^{\prime}}^{N}=\chi_{x}(\alpha_{\partial})
. Since
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}(\alpha_{\partial_{1}}\otimes\alpha_{\partial_{2}})=\alpha_{\partial}
, one can find (and do fix)
h_{\partial_{1}},h_{\partial_{2}}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
h_{\partial}=h_{\partial_{1}}h_{\partial_{2}}
and
h_{\partial_{1}}^{N}=\chi_{x_{1}}(\alpha_{\partial_{1}})
,
h_{\partial_{2}}^{N}=\chi_{x_{2}}(\alpha_{\partial_{2}})
. Define
(\widehat{x}_{1},\widehat{x}_{2})\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
by, for
i=1,2
,
\widehat{x}_{i}=(x_{i},h_{p},h_{\partial})_{p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{i},\partial\in\Gamma_{i}^{\partial}}
where for
p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{i}\ \subset\mathring{\mathcal{P}}
,
h_{p}
is the puncture invariant of
\widehat{x}
, for
\partial\in\Gamma_{i}^{\partial}\setminus\{\partial_{i}\}
then
h_{\partial}
is the boundary invariant of
\widehat{x}
and
h_{\partial_{i}}
is the previously chosen scalar. Then
\widehat{p}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}(\widehat{x}_{1},\widehat{x}_{2})=\widehat{x}
, thus
\widehat{p}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
is surjective.

∎

4.
Representations of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1})

Let
\mathbb{D}_{1}=(\Sigma_{0,2},\{a_{L},a_{R}\})
be an annulus with two boundary edges in the same boundary component (i.e. a punctured bigon). It admits a presenting graph with two edges
\alpha,\beta
represented in Figure
8
(note that
\alpha
and
\beta^{-1}
are corner arcs). The algebra
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is isomorphic to the Drinfeld double of the quantum Borel algebra
D_{q}B
described as follows.

Figure 8.
Two oriented arcs
\alpha
and
\beta
in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
.

Definition 4.1

(Quantum enveloping algebra)
.

Write
q:=A^{2}
. The algebra
D_{q}B
is presented by the generators
K^{\pm 1/2},L^{\pm 1/2},E
and
F
together with the following relations:

\displaystyle EK^{1/2}=q^{-1}K^{1/2}E;\quad EL^{1/2}=qL^{1/2}E;\quad FK^{1/2}=qK^{1/2}F;\quad FL^{1/2}=q^{-1}L^{1/2}F;

\displaystyle xy=yx\mbox{, for all }x,y\in\{K^{\pm 1/2},L^{\pm 1/2}\};\quad K^{1/2}K^{-1/2}=L^{1/2}L^{-1/2}=1;

\displaystyle EF-FE=\frac{K-L}{q-q^{-1}}.

Theorem 4.2
.

(
[
Kor19a
]
)
There is an isomorphism of algebras
\Psi:D_{q}B\xrightarrow{\cong}\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
defined by

\displaystyle\Psi(K^{1/2})=\alpha_{--},\quad\Psi(K^{-1/2})=\alpha_{++},\quad\Psi(L^{1/2})=\beta_{--},\quad\Psi(L^{-1/2})=\beta_{++}

\displaystyle\Psi(E)=\frac{-A}{q-q^{-1}}\alpha_{+-}\alpha_{--},\quad\Psi(F)=\frac{A^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}\beta_{--}\beta_{-+}

Note that
H_{\partial}:=K^{-1/2}L^{-1/2}
is a central element such that
\Psi(H_{\partial})=h_{\partial}
for
\partial
the connected component containing
a_{L}
and
a_{R}
. Also, if
p
denotes the only inner puncture of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, then

(3)

C:=-\frac{\Psi^{-1}(\gamma_{p})H_{\partial}^{-1}}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}=EF+\frac{qL+q^{-1}K}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}=FE+\frac{qK+q^{-1}L}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}

is the Casimir element of
D_{q}B
. Let
\widetilde{U}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$D_{q}B$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(H_{\partial}-1)$}\right.}
and
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\subset\widetilde{U}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
the subalgebra generated by
K^{\pm 1},E
and
F
. The simple modules of
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
have been classified in
[
DCK89
,
AR89
]
(see also
[
KS97
, Chapter
1
Section
3.3
]
) whereas many indecomposable semi-weight
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules were found and studied in
[
Arn94
]
. We now provide a classification of the indecomposable (semi) weight
D_{q}B
modules as follows.

Definition 4.3

(Weight representations of
D_{q}B
)
.

We define three families of indecomposable weight
D_{q}B
-modules as follows. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
[n]:=\frac{q^{n}-q^{-n}}{q-q^{-1}}
.

(1)

For
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
, the module
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
has canonical basis
(v_{0},\ldots,v_{N-1})
and module structure given by:

\displaystyle K^{1/2}v_{i}=\lambda q^{-i}v_{i};\quad L^{1/2}v_{i}=\mu q^{i}v_{i}\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Fv_{N-1}=bv_{0};\quad Fv_{i}=v_{i+1}\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\};

\displaystyle Ev_{0}=av_{N-1};\quad Ev_{i}=\left(\frac{q^{-i+1}\lambda^{2}-q^{i-1}\mu^{2}}{q-q^{-1}}[i]+ab\right)v_{i-1}\mbox{, for }i\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}.

(2)

For
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
c\in\mathbb{C}
, the module
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
has canonical basis
(w_{0},\ldots,w_{N-1})
and module structure given by:

\displaystyle K^{1/2}w_{i}=\lambda q^{i}w_{i};\quad L^{1/2}w_{i}=\mu q^{-i}w_{i}\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Ew_{N-1}=cw_{0};\quad Ew_{i}=w_{i+1}\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\};

\displaystyle Fw_{0}=0;\quad Fw_{i}=\left(\frac{\mu^{2}q^{-i+1}-\lambda^{2}q^{i-1}}{q-q^{-1}}[i]\right)w_{i}\mbox{, for }i\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}.

(3)

For
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
,
0\leq n\leq N-1
, the module
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
has canonical basis
(e_{0},\ldots,e_{n})
and module structure given by:

\displaystyle K^{1/2}e_{i}=\varepsilon\mu A^{n-2i}e_{i};\quad L^{1/2}e_{i}=\mu A^{2i-n}e_{i}\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,n\};

\displaystyle Fe_{n}=0;\quad Fe_{i}=e_{i+1}\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\};

\displaystyle Ee_{0}=0;\quad Ee_{i}=\mu^{2}[i][n-i+1]e_{i-1}\mbox{, for }i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

Definition 4.4

(Standard semi-weight representations of
D_{q}B
)
.

We define three families of indecomposable semi-weight representations as follows.

(1)

For
\lambda,\mu,b\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
a\in\mathbb{C}
such that

h_{p}:=-(q-q^{-1})^{2}(\lambda\mu)^{-1}ab-\lambda\mu^{-1}q-\mu\lambda^{-1}q^{-1}=\pm(q^{n}+q^{-n})\quad\mbox{ for some }n\in\{1,\ldots,(N-1)/2\}

the module
P(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
has canonical basis
(x_{0},\ldots,x_{N-1},y_{0},\ldots,y_{N-1})
and module structure given by:

\displaystyle K^{1/2}x_{i}=\lambda q^{-i}x_{i},K^{1/2}y_{i}=\lambda q^{-i}y_{i},L^{1/2}x_{i}=\mu q^{i}x_{i},L^{1/2}y_{i}=\mu q^{i}y_{i}\mbox{ for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Fx_{N-1}=bx_{0},Fy_{N-1}=by_{0},Fx_{i}=x_{i+1},Fy_{i}=y_{i+1}\mbox{ for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\};

\displaystyle Ex_{0}=ax_{N-1},Ex_{i}=\left(\frac{q^{-i+1}\lambda^{2}-q^{i-1}\mu^{2}}{q-q^{-1}}[i]+ab\right)x_{i-1}\mbox{ for }i\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Ey_{0}=ay_{N-1}+b^{-1}x_{N-1},Ey_{i}=\left(\frac{q^{-i+1}\lambda^{2}-q^{i-1}\mu^{2}}{q-q^{-1}}[i]+ab\right)y_{i-1}+x_{i-1}\mbox{ for }i\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}.

(2)

For
\lambda,\mu,c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
\lambda\mu^{-1}=\pm q^{n}
for some
n\in\{1,\ldots,(N-1)/2\}
, the module
\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c)
has canonical basis
(x_{0},\ldots,x_{N-1},y_{0},\ldots,y_{N-1})
and module structure given by:

\displaystyle K^{1/2}x_{i}=\lambda q^{i}x_{i},K^{1/2}y_{i}=\lambda q^{i}y_{i},L^{1/2}x_{i}=\mu q^{-i}x_{i},L^{1/2}y_{i}=\mu q^{-i}y_{i}\mbox{ for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Ex_{N-1}=cx_{0},Ey_{N-1}=cy_{0},Ex_{i}=x_{i+1},Ey_{i}=y_{i+1}\mbox{ for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\};

\displaystyle Fx_{0}=0,Fx_{i}=\left(\frac{\mu^{2}q^{-i+1}-\lambda^{2}q^{i-1}}{q-q^{-1}}[i]\right)x_{i-1}\mbox{ for }i\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Fy_{0}=x_{0},Fy_{i}=\left(\frac{\mu^{2}q^{-i+1}-\lambda^{2}q^{i-1}}{q-q^{-1}}[i]\right)y_{i-1}+x_{i}\mbox{ for }i\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}.

(3)

For
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
,
0\leq n\leq N-2
, the module
P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
has canonical basis
(x_{0},\ldots,x_{N-1},y_{0},\ldots,y_{N-1})
and module structure given by:

\displaystyle K^{1/2}x_{i}=\varepsilon\mu A^{-2-n-2i}x_{i},K^{1/2}y_{i}=\varepsilon\mu A^{n-2i}y_{i},\quad L^{1/2}x_{i}=\mu A^{n+2i+2}x_{i},L^{1/2}y_{i}=\mu A^{2i-n}y_{i}\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Fx_{i}=x_{i+1},\quad Fy_{i}=y_{i+1}\quad\mbox{( where }y_{N}=x_{N}=0)\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Ex_{0}=0,\quad Ex_{i}=-\mu^{2}[i][n+i+1]x_{i-1}\mbox{, for }i\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\};

\displaystyle Ey_{i}=\mu^{2}[i][n-i+1]y_{i-1}+x_{N-n+i-2}\mbox{, for }i\in\{0,\ldots,n\},\quad Ey_{i}=\mu^{2}[i][n-i+1]y_{i-1}\mbox{, for }i\in\{n+1,\ldots,N-1\}.

Definition 4.5

(Exceptional semi-weight representations)
.

For
0\leq n\leq N-1
, write
\overline{n}:=N-2-n
.

(1)

For
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
,
0\leq n\leq N-2
,
k\geq 1
, the module
\Omega^{-k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
has basis
(e_{i}^{j},\overline{e}_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}},0\leq i\leq n,0\leq i^{\prime}\leq\overline{n},1\leq j\leq k,1\leq j^{\prime}\leq k+1)
and module structure given by:

\displaystyle K^{1/2}e_{i}^{j}=\varepsilon\mu A^{n-2i}e_{i}^{j},K^{1/2}\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\varepsilon\mu A^{\overline{n}-2i}\overline{e}_{i}^{j},L^{1/2}e_{i}^{j}=\mu A^{2i-n}e_{i}^{j},L^{1/2}\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\mu A^{2i-\overline{n}}\overline{e}_{i}^{j};

\displaystyle Fe_{n}^{j}=\overline{e}_{0}^{j+1},F\overline{e}_{\overline{n}}^{j}=0,Fe_{i}^{j}=e_{i+1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq n,F\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\overline{e}_{i+1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq\overline{n};

\displaystyle Ee_{0}^{j}=\overline{e}_{\overline{n}}^{j},E\overline{e}_{0}^{j}=0,Ee_{i}^{j}=\mu^{2}[i][n-i+1]e_{i-1}^{j},E\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\mu^{2}[i][\overline{n}-i+1]\overline{e}_{i-1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq 0.

(2)

For
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
,
0\leq n\leq N-2
,
k\geq 1
, the module
\Omega^{k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
has basis
(e_{i}^{j},\overline{e}_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}},0\leq i\leq n,0\leq i^{\prime}\leq\overline{n},1\leq j\leq k+1,1\leq j^{\prime}\leq k)
and module structure given by:

\displaystyle K^{1/2}e_{i}^{j}=\varepsilon\mu A^{n-2i}e_{i}^{j},K^{1/2}\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\varepsilon\mu A^{\overline{n}-2i}\overline{e}_{i}^{j},L^{1/2}e_{i}^{j}=\mu A^{2i-n}e_{i}^{j},L^{1/2}\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\mu A^{2i-\overline{n}}\overline{e}_{i}^{j};

\displaystyle Fe_{n}^{k+1}=0,Fe_{n}^{j}=\overline{e}_{0}^{j}\mbox{ for }j\neq k+1,F\overline{e}_{\overline{n}}^{j}=0,Fe_{i}^{j}=e_{i+1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq n,F\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\overline{e}_{i+1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq\overline{n};

\displaystyle Ee_{0}^{1}=0,Ee_{0}^{j}=\overline{e}_{\overline{n}}^{j-1}\mbox{ for }j\neq 1,E\overline{e}_{0}^{j}=0,Ee_{i}^{j}=\mu^{2}[i][n-i+1]e_{i-1}^{j},E\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\mu^{2}[i][\overline{n}-i+1]\overline{e}_{i-1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq 0.

(3)

For
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
,
0\leq n\leq N-2
,
k\geq 1
and
\lambda=\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
, the module
M^{k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}(\lambda)
has basis
(e_{i}^{j},\overline{e}_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}},0\leq i\leq n,0\leq i^{\prime}\leq\overline{n},1\leq j\leq k,1\leq j^{\prime}\leq k)
and module structure given as follows.

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
then

\displaystyle K^{1/2}e_{i}^{j}=\varepsilon\mu A^{n-2i}e_{i}^{j},K^{1/2}\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\varepsilon\mu A^{\overline{n}-2i}\overline{e}_{i}^{j},L^{1/2}e_{i}^{j}=\mu A^{2i-n}e_{i}^{j},L^{1/2}\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\mu A^{2i-\overline{n}}\overline{e}_{i}^{j};

\displaystyle Fe_{n}^{k}=\lambda\overline{e}_{0}^{k},Fe_{n}^{j}=\lambda\overline{e}_{0}^{j}+\overline{e}_{0}^{j+1}\mbox{ for }j\neq k,F\overline{e}_{\overline{n}}^{j}=0,Fe_{i}^{j}=e_{i+1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq n,F\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\overline{e}_{i+1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq\overline{n};

\displaystyle Ee_{0}^{j}=\overline{e}_{\overline{n}}^{j},E\overline{e}_{0}^{j}=0,Ee_{i}^{j}=\mu^{2}[i][n-i+1]e_{i-1}^{j},E\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\mu^{2}[i][\overline{n}-i+1]\overline{e}_{i-1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq 0.

If
\lambda=\infty\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}
, then

\displaystyle K^{1/2}e_{i}^{j}=\varepsilon\mu A^{n-2i}e_{i}^{j},K^{1/2}\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\varepsilon\mu A^{\overline{n}-2i}\overline{e}_{i}^{j},L^{1/2}e_{i}^{j}=\mu A^{2i-n}e_{i}^{j},L^{1/2}\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\mu A^{2i-\overline{n}}\overline{e}_{i}^{j};

\displaystyle Fe_{n}^{j}=\overline{e}_{0}^{j},F\overline{e}_{\overline{n}}^{j}=0,Fe_{i}^{j}=e_{i+1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq n,F\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\overline{e}_{i+1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq\overline{n};

\displaystyle Ee_{0}^{k}=0,Ee_{0}^{j}=\overline{e}_{\overline{n}}^{j+1}\mbox{ for }j\neq k,E\overline{e}_{0}^{j}=0,Ee_{i}^{j}=\mu^{2}[i][n-i+1]e_{i-1}^{j},E\overline{e}_{i}^{j}=\mu^{2}[i][\overline{n}-i+1]\overline{e}_{i-1}^{j}\mbox{ for }i\neq 0.

We postpone the proof of the following theorem to the next subsection. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of weight
D_{q}B
modules and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
the category of semi-weight
D_{q}B
modules.

Theorem 4.6
.

(1)

Every modules
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b),\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c),S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
are weight indecomposable and every weight indecomposable
D_{q}B
module is isomorphic to one of them.

(2)

Every modules
P(\lambda,\mu,a,b),\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c),P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},\Omega^{k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},\Omega^{-k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},M_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}^{k}(\lambda)
are semi weight indecomposable and every indecomposable semi-weight module is either isomorphic to one of them or is a weight module.

(3)

The modules
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
are simple. The module
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
is simple if and only if either
c\neq 0
or
\lambda\mu^{-1}\neq\pm q^{n-1}
for all
n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
. The module
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
is simple if and only if either
\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}}(ab+\frac{q^{1-i}\lambda^{2}-q^{i-1}\mu^{2}}{q-q^{-1}}[i])\neq 0
or
\lambda\mu^{-1}\neq\pm q^{n-1}
for all
n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
. Any simple
D_{q}B
-module is isomorphic to one of these modules.

(4)

The weight representations
S_{\mu,\epsilon,N-1}
,
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
and
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
are projective objects in
\mathcal{C}
and any indecomposable projective object of
\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to one of them.

(5)

The semi weight representations
S_{\mu,\epsilon,N-1}
,
P_{\mu,\epsilon,n}
,
P(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
and
\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c)
are projective objects in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and any indecomposable projective object of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is isomorphic to one of them.

Let us identify
X(\mathbb{D}_{1})
with the set of pairs
g=(g_{+},g_{-})
where
g_{+}\in B^{+}
and
g_{-}\in B^{-}
are upper and lower triangular matrices of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. More precisely, for
x\in X(\mathbb{D}_{1})
, we set

\displaystyle g_{+}:=\begin{pmatrix}\chi_{x}(\alpha_{++}^{N})&\chi_{x}(\alpha_{+-}^{N})\\
0&\chi_{x}(\alpha_{--}^{N})\end{pmatrix}=\chi_{x}\begin{pmatrix}K^{-N/2}&-(q-q^{-1})^{N}E^{N}K^{-N/2}\\
0&K^{N/2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle g_{-}:=\begin{pmatrix}\chi_{x}(\beta_{++}^{N})&0\\
\chi_{x}(\beta_{-+}^{N})&\chi_{x}(\beta_{--}^{N})\end{pmatrix}=\chi_{x}\begin{pmatrix}L^{-N/2}&0\\
(q-q^{-1})^{N}L^{-N/2}F^{N}&L^{N/2}\end{pmatrix}.

Write
\varphi(g):=g_{-}^{-1}g_{+}
.
\widehat{X}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is identified with the set of elements
(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})
with
g=(g_{+},g_{-})\in X(\mathbb{D}_{1})
,
T_{N}(\gamma_{p})=-\operatorname{tr}(\varphi(g))
and
h_{\partial}^{-N}
is the left upper matrix coefficient of
g_{-}g_{+}
. An indecomposable representation of weight
(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})
is called cyclic/ semi-cyclic/ diagonal/ central if
\varphi(g)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is not triangular/ triangular not diagonal/ diagonal not central/ central (i.e.
\varphi(g)=\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
) respectively.

Notations 4.7
.

Write
\Delta_{+}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}):=\{(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}|(n-m)^{2}\leq 1\}
and decompose it as

\Delta_{+}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})=\{(0,1),(1,0)\}\sqcup\Delta^{\Re}_{++}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})\sqcup\Delta^{Im}_{+}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
where

\Delta^{\Re}_{++}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})=\{(k,k+1),k\geq 1\}\cup\{(k+1,k),k\geq 1\},\quad\Delta^{Im}_{+}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})=\{(k,k),k\geq 1\}.

Further write

\Delta:=\{S,P\}\sqcup\Delta^{\Re}_{++}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})\sqcup\Delta^{Im}_{+}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}.

Given
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
,
n\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\}
, define a map
\underline{\sigma}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}:\Delta\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(U_{q}\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
by

\underline{\sigma}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}:S\mapsto S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},P\mapsto P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},(k+1,k)\mapsto\Omega^{k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},(k,k+1)\mapsto\Omega^{-k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},((k,k),\Lambda)\mapsto M^{k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}(\Lambda).

Consider the set
\Delta\sqcup\overline{\Delta}
made of two copies of
\Delta
; said differently
\Delta\sqcup\overline{\Delta}:=\Delta\times\{0,1\}
and for
\alpha\in\Delta
we write
\alpha:=(\alpha,0)\in\Delta\bigsqcup\overline{\Delta}
and
\overline{\alpha}:=(\alpha,1)\in\Delta\sqcup\overline{\Delta}
. For
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
,
n\in\{0,\ldots,(N-3)/2\}
, recall that
\overline{n}=N-n-2
and consider the map

\sigma_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}:=\underline{\sigma}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}\sqcup\underline{\sigma}_{\mu,\varepsilon,\overline{n}}:\Delta\sqcup\overline{\Delta}\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(D_{q}B).

As a consequence of Theorem
4.6
, we obtain the

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
be the category of finite dimensional semi-weight
D_{q}
modules.

(1)

The Azumaya locus of
D_{q}B\cong\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is the set of elements
(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})
such that either
\varphi(g)
is not central or
\varphi(g)=\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
and
h_{p}=\mp 2
so it is equal to the regular locus
\widehat{X}^{reg}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
.

(2)

The fully Azumaya locus of
D_{q}B\cong\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is the set of elements
g\in X(\mathbb{D}_{1})
such that
\varphi(g)\neq\pm\mathds{1}
. For such a
g
, the isomorphism classes of semi-weight indecomposable
D_{q}
modules with classical shadow
g
are completely determined by their full shadow.

(3)

For
\widehat{g}=(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})\in\widehat{X}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
with
\varphi(g)\neq\pm\mathds{1}
then

•

If
\operatorname{tr}(\varphi(g))\neq\pm 2
, then up to isomorphism there is a unique indecomposable semi-weight module representation
S_{\widehat{g}}
with maximal shadow
\widehat{g}
: it is the Azumaya representation which is both simple and projective in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.

•

If
\operatorname{tr}(\varphi(g))=\pm 2
, then up to isomorphism there exist two indecomposable semi-weight representations
S_{\widehat{g}},P_{\widehat{g}}
with maximal shadow
\widehat{g}
.
S_{\widehat{g}}
is the Azumaya representation and is simple not projective.
P_{\widehat{g}}
is projective in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
not simple and we have a non split exact sequence

0\to S_{\widehat{g}}\to P_{\widehat{g}}\to S_{\widehat{g}}\to 0.

(4)

Let
\widehat{x}=(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})\in\widehat{X}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
which is not in the Azumaya locus, so one has
g=\left(\varepsilon\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda&0\\
0&\Lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda&0\\
0&\Lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)
for some
\varepsilon=\pm 1
and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
h_{p}=-\varepsilon(q^{n+1}+q^{-(1+n)})
for some
n\in\{0,\ldots,(N-3)/2\}
. Let
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
be the unique scalar such that
\mu^{-N}=\Lambda
and
h_{\partial}=\varepsilon\mu^{-2}
. Then
\sigma_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
is a bijection between
\Delta\sqcup\overline{\Delta}
and the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable
D_{q}B
modules with classical shadow
\widehat{x}
.

In particular, Theorem
1.2
is verified for
\mathbb{D}_{1}
.

Remark 4.9
.

Being a projective object in the category
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
of semi-weight modules is different from being a projective module (i.e. from being a projective object in
D_{q}B-\operatorname{Mod}
). As we shall see, for
\widehat{g}=(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})\in\widehat{X}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
with
\operatorname{tr}(\varphi(g))\neq\pm 2
, the Azumaya representation
S_{\widehat{g}}
is a proper submodule of an indecomposable module of the form
P(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
which is not semi-weight. So
S_{\widehat{g}}

is not a projective module though it is a projective object in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.

4.1.
Classification of semi-weight
D_{q}B
modules

In this subsection, we prove Theorem
4.6
. Denote by
\mathcal{C}
the category of weight
D_{q}B
modules and by
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
its category of semi-weight modules. For
0\leq n\leq N-1
, we write
\overline{n}:=N-n-2
.

4.1.1.
Representations in the fully Azumaya locus

Lemma 4.10
.

(1)

For
0\leq n\leq N-2
, we have a non-split exact sequence

(4)

0\rightarrow S_{\mu,\varepsilon,\overline{n}}\xrightarrow{i}V(\varepsilon\mu A^{n},\mu A^{-n},0,0)\xrightarrow{p}S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}\rightarrow 0,

where the equivariant maps
i
and
p
are defined by
i(e_{j}):=v_{j+n+1}
for
0\leq j\leq N-n-2
and
p(v_{j}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}e_{j}&\mbox{, if }0\leq i\leq n;\\
0&\mbox{, else.}\end{array}\right.

(2)

One has non-split exact sequences:

0\to V(\varepsilon\mu A^{-2-n},\mu A^{2+n},0,0)\xrightarrow{\iota}P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}\xrightarrow{p}V(\varepsilon\mu A^{n},\mu A^{-n},0,0)\to 0,

0\to V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)\xrightarrow{\iota}P(\lambda,\mu,a,b)\xrightarrow{p}V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)\to 0,

and

0\to\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)\xrightarrow{\iota}\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c)\xrightarrow{p}\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)\to 0,

where
\iota(v_{i}):=x_{i}
and
p(x_{i}):=0
,
p(y_{i}):=v_{i}
(resp.
\iota(w_{i})=x_{i}
,
p(x_{i})=0
,
p(y_{i})=w_{i}
in the third case).

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward verification left to the reader. ∎

For
(\lambda,\mu,b)\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
a\in\mathbb{C}
, the module
P(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
of Definition
4.4
is always well defined. Also for
c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c)
is well defined as well. However they are not always semi weight modules.

Lemma 4.11
.

(1)

P(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
is a semi weight module if and only if

h_{p}:=-(q-q^{-1})^{2}(\lambda\mu)^{-1}ab-\lambda\mu^{-1}q-\mu\lambda^{-1}q^{-1}=\pm(q^{n}+q^{-n})\quad\mbox{ for some }n\in\{1,\ldots,(N-1)/2\}

(2)

\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c)
is a semi weight module if and only if
\lambda\mu^{-1}=\pm q^{n}
for some
n\in\{1,\ldots,(N-1)/2\}
.

(3)

P_{\lambda,\varepsilon,n}
is always a semi weight module.

Proof.

Denote by
\rho:D_{q}B\to\operatorname{End}(P(\lambda,\mu,a,b))
the associated representation. Clearly we have
\rho(K^{N/2})=\lambda^{N}id
,
\rho(L^{N/2})=\mu^{N}id
,
\rho(F^{N})=bid
.
Let
V:=\operatorname{Span}(x_{i},i=0,\ldots,N-1)\subset P(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
such that
V\cong V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
and denote by
\widehat{x}=(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})
its classical shadow.
In particular:

h_{p}:=-(q-q^{-1})^{2}(\lambda\mu)^{-1}ab-\lambda\mu^{-1}q-\mu\lambda^{-1}q^{-1}

and
\mathchoice{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=1.65279pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=1.65279pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=1.15694pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=1.44028pt}}_{\,V}}(\gamma_{p})=h_{p}id_{V}
. Write

e:=-\frac{(\lambda\mu)^{N}}{(q-q^{-1})^{2N}}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}}(h_{p}+\lambda\mu^{-1}q^{1-2i}+\mu\lambda^{-1}q^{2i-1}).

Clearly
\mathchoice{{\rho(E^{N})\,\smash{\vrule height=7.12982pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho(E^{N})\,\smash{\vrule height=6.72983pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho(E^{N})\,\smash{\vrule height=5.1476pt,depth=1.4875pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho(E^{N})\,\smash{\vrule height=3.8889pt,depth=1.44028pt}}_{\,V}}=eid_{V}
. Let us prove that
\rho(E^{N})=eid
if and only if
h_{p}\in\{\pm(q^{n}+q^{-n}),n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}\}
. Write

e_{i}(X):=-\frac{\lambda\mu}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}(X+\lambda\mu^{-1}q^{1-2i}+\mu\lambda^{-1}q^{2i-1})

and
P(X):=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}}e_{i}(X)-e
so that
P(h_{p})=0
. Using the equality

\rho(E)y_{i}=e_{i}(h_{p})y_{i-1}+x_{i-1},

we find that

\rho(E^{N})y_{i}=ey_{i}+P^{\prime}(h_{p})x_{i}.

So
\rho(E^{N})=eid
if and only if
h_{p}
is a zero of
P(X)
of multiplicity
>1
. On the other hand, since the minimal polynomial of
\mathchoice{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=1.65279pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=1.65279pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=1.15694pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=1.44028pt}}_{\,V}}(\gamma_{p})
is
T_{N}(X)+\operatorname{tr}(\varphi(g))
and since
P(\mathchoice{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=1.65279pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=1.65279pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=1.15694pt}}_{\,V}}{{\rho\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=1.44028pt}}_{\,V}}(\gamma_{p}))=0
, we have
P(X)=-\frac{(\lambda\mu)^{N}}{(q-q^{-1})^{2N}}(T_{N}(X)+\operatorname{tr}(\varphi(g)))
so
h_{p}
is a root or
P(X)
of multiplicity
>1
if and only if
h_{p}\in\{\pm(q^{n}+q^{-n}),n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}\}
.

The cases of
\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c)
and
P_{\lambda,\varepsilon,n}
are done similarly (and much easier) and left to the reader.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

(1)

The modules
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},V(\lambda,\mu,a,b),\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c),P(\lambda,\mu,a,b),\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c),P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
are indecomposable.

(2)

The modules
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
are simple. The module
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
is simple if and only if either
c\neq 0
or
\lambda\mu^{-1}\neq\pm q^{n-1}
for all
n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
. The module
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
is simple if and only if either
\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}}(ab+\frac{q^{1-i}\lambda^{2}-q^{i-1}\mu^{2}}{q-q^{-1}}[i])\neq 0
or
\lambda\mu^{-1}\neq\pm q^{n-1}
for all
n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
.

Proof.

Let
V
be one module of the form
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},V(\lambda,\mu,a,b),\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
and denote by
(u_{i})_{i}
its canonical basis (
i.e.

u_{i}
is either
e_{i}
,
v_{i}
or
w_{i}
if
V
has the form
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
or
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
respectively). Write
\rho:D_{q}B\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(V)
the morphism associated to the module structure. Let
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)
be the space of equivariant endomorphisms of
V
and fix
0\neq\theta\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)
. By Definition
4.3
, the operator
\rho(K^{1/2})
is diagonal in the basis
(u_{i})_{i}
and each eigenvalue arises with multiplicity one. Since
\theta
commutes with
\rho(K^{1/2})
, it preserves the eigenspace
\mathbb{C}u_{0}
, hence there exists
z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\theta\cdot u_{0}=zu_{0}
. First suppose that
z\neq 0
. By definition, the vectors of the canonical basis are characterized by either
u_{i}=\rho(F)^{i}u_{0}
or
u_{i}=\rho(E)^{i}u_{0}
. Since
\theta
commutes with both
\rho(E)
and
\rho(F)
, one has
\theta u_{i}=zu_{i}
and
\theta
is scalar. Next suppose that
\theta u_{0}=0
. Since
\theta\neq 0
, there exists an index
i_{0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, such that
\theta u_{i_{0}}=zu_{i_{0}}
and
\theta u_{i}=0
for all
0\leq i\leq i_{0}-1
. Let
W:=\operatorname{Span}\left(u_{j},j\geq i_{0}\right)\subset V
and denote by
P_{W}
the projection onto
W
parallel to
\operatorname{Span}\left(u_{j},0\leq j\leq i_{0}\right)
. Since the vectors
u_{j}
, for
j\geq i_{0}
are obtained from
u_{i_{0}}
by composition with some power of either
\rho(E)
or
\rho(F)
, the equivariance of
\theta
implies that
\theta=zP_{W}
.

Conversely, if there exists such an equivariant projector
P_{W}
, then one has either
\rho(E)u_{i_{0}}=0
, if
V
has the form
V_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
or
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
, and
\rho(F)u_{i_{0}}=0
, if
V
has the form
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
. By Definition
4.3
, such an index
i_{0}
does not exist for the modules
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
, neither for the modules
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
such that either
c\neq 0
or
\lambda\mu^{-1}\neq\pm q^{n-1}
for all
n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
, nor for the modules
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
such that either
\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}}(ab+\frac{q^{1-i}\lambda^{2}-q^{i-1}\mu^{2}}{q-q^{-1}}[i])\neq 0
or
\lambda\mu^{-1}\neq\pm q^{n-1}
for all
n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
. Hence in each of these cases, one has
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)=\mathbb{C}id
and these modules are simple. In the other cases, one finds that such an index
i_{0}
is unique and by Lemmas
4.10
, the corresponding projector
P_{W}
is equivariant. Hence
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)=id\oplus P_{W}
. Moreover in this case,
V
is isomorphic to a module
V(\varepsilon\mu A^{n},\mu A^{-n},0,0)

and since the exact sequence (
4
) in Lemma
4.10
does not split,
V
is indecomposable. Similarly, the fact that the modules
P(\lambda,\mu,a,b),\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c)
are indecomposable follows from the existence of the non-split exact sequences in Lemma
4.10
.

Let us prove that
P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
is indecomposable. Let
\pi:=\rho(C-\frac{\mu^{2}}{q-q^{-1}}[n+1])\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n})
be the nilpotent map such that
\pi(x_{j})=0
,
\pi(y_{i})=x_{N-n-i}
for
i\leq n-1
,
\pi(y_{i})=0
for
i\geq n
, and let us prove that
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n})=id\oplus\pi
. This will imply that
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n})\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[\pi]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\pi^{2})$}\right.}
is a local ring, thus that
P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
is indecomposable.
Let
\theta\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n})
. Then
\theta(y_{0})
is an eigenvector of
K^{1/2}
with eigenvalue
\varepsilon\mu A^{n}
and is annihilated by
(FE)^{2}
, so there exists
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\theta(y_{0})=cy_{0}+c^{\prime}x_{N-n}
. Let us prove that
\theta=cid+c^{\prime}\pi
. First, since
\theta(F^{i}y_{0})=F^{i}\theta(y_{0})
, one finds:

\theta(y_{i})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}cy_{i}+c^{\prime}x_{N-n+i}&\mbox{, if }i\leq n-1;\\
cy_{i}&\mbox{ if }i\geq n.\end{array}\right.=(cid+c^{\prime}\pi)(y_{i}).

Next, since
\theta(Ey_{0})=E\theta(y_{0})
one finds
\theta(x_{N-n-1})=cx_{N-n-1}=(cid+c^{\prime}\pi)x_{N-n-1}
. Since
\theta(F^{i}x_{N-n-1})=F^{i}\theta(x_{N-n-1})
, one gets
\theta(x_{i})=cx_{i}=(cid+c^{\prime}\pi)(x_{i})
for
i\geq N-n-1
and since
\theta(E^{i}x_{N-n-1})=E^{i}\theta(x_{N-n-i})
one finds that
\theta(x_{i})=cx_{i}=(cid+c^{\prime}\pi)x_{i}
for
i\leq N-n-1
. Thus
\theta=cid+c^{\prime}\pi
.

∎

Lemma 4.13
.

(1)

The Azumaya locus
\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is the set of elements
(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})
such that either
\varphi(g)\neq\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
or
\varphi(g)=\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
and
h_{p}=\mp 2
.

(2)

The fully Azumaya locus
\mathcal{FAL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is the set of elements
g
such that
\varphi(g)\neq\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
.

Proof.

The second assertion is an immediate consequence of the first one. To compute
\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
, recall from Theorem
2.20
that an irreducible representation
\rho:D_{q}B\to\operatorname{End}(V)
with classical shadow
\widehat{x}
has dimension
N
if and only
\widehat{x}\in\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
. When
\widehat{x}=(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})
is such that
\varphi(g)\neq\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
, then it is the classical shadow of an
N
-dimensional representation of the form
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
or
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
which is irreducible by Lemma
4.12
. When
\varphi(g)=\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
and
h_{p}=\mp 2
, then
\widehat{x}
is the shadow of an
N
dimensional representation
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,N-1}
which is irreducible by Lemma
4.12
. So
\widehat{x}\in\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
in these cases. If
\varphi(g)=\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
and
h_{p}\neq\mp 2
, then
\widehat{x}
is the shadow of a representation
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}
with
0\leq n\leq N-2
whose dimension if
<N
and which is irreducible by Lemma
4.12
, so
\widehat{x}\notin\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
.

∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
\widehat{x}=(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})\in\widehat{X}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
.

(1)

If
\operatorname{tr}(\varphi(g))\neq\pm 2
, there exists (up to isomorphism) a unique indecomposable semi-weight representation with maximal shadow
\widehat{x}
; it is of the form
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
or
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
.

(2)

If
\operatorname{tr}(\varphi(g))=\pm 2
and
\varphi(g)\neq\mp\mathds{1}_{2}
, there exist (up to isomorphism) two indecomposable semi-weight representations with maximal shadow
\widehat{x}
; one is of the form
V(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
or
\widetilde{V}(\lambda,\mu,c)
and the other has the form
P(\lambda,\mu,a,b)
or
\widetilde{P}(\lambda,\mu,c)
.

Proof.

When
\varphi(g)\neq\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
, then
g\in\mathcal{FAL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
by Lemma
4.13
so the lemma follows from Theorem
2.30

∎

4.1.2.
Representations outside the fully Azumaya locus

For
\mathscr{A}
an algebra, we denote by
\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathscr{A})
the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable
\mathscr{A}
-modules.

Definition 4.15
.

(Small quantum group) The (simply connected)
small quantum enveloping algebra

\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
has generators
E,F,k^{\pm 1/2}
and relations:

Ek^{1/2}=q^{-1}k^{1/2}E;\quad Fk^{1/2}=qk^{1/2}F;\quad F^{N}=E^{N}=k^{N/2}-1=0,\quad EF-FE=\frac{k-k^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}.

Let
\rho:D_{q}B\to\operatorname{End}(V)
be an indecomposable semi-weight representation whose shadow
g\in X(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is not in the fully Azumaya locus. By Lemma
4.13
,
g=\left(\varepsilon\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda&0\\
0&\Lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda&0\\
0&\Lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)
for some
\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}
and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
.
Since
\rho
is indecomposable, there exists
h_{\partial}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
\rho(H_{\partial})=h_{\partial}id_{V}
. Let
\mu\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
be the unique scalar such that
\Lambda=\mu^{N}
and
h_{\partial}=\varepsilon\mu^{-2}
and denote by
\operatorname{Indecomp}(U_{q}\mathfrak{gl}_{2};\varepsilon,\mu)\subset\operatorname{Indecomp}(D_{q}B)
the subset of modules with shadow
g
and
h_{\partial}
. Define a map

(\cdot)_{\mu,\varepsilon}:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(D_{q}B;\varepsilon,\mu),\quad V\mapsto V_{\mu,\varepsilon},

by sending
\rho:\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
to the representation
\rho_{\mu,\varepsilon}:D_{q}B\to\operatorname{End}(V_{\mu,\varepsilon})
where
V_{\mu,\varepsilon}=V
as a vector space and

\rho_{\mu,\varepsilon}(K^{1/2})=\mu\varepsilon\rho(k^{1/2}),\rho_{\mu,\varepsilon}(L^{1/2})=\mu\rho(k^{-1/2}),\rho_{\mu,\varepsilon}(E)=\mu^{2}\rho(E),\rho_{\mu,\varepsilon}(F)=\rho(F).

Lemma 4.16
.

(\cdot)_{\mu,\varepsilon}:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(D_{q}B;\varepsilon,\mu)
is a bijection.

Proof.

The construction of the inverse map is straightforward and left to the reader.
∎

Notations 4.17
.

We denote by
S_{n},P_{n},\Omega^{\pm k}_{n},M^{k}_{n}(\Lambda)
the
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules which correspond to the modules
S_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},P_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},\Omega^{\pm k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n},M^{k}_{\mu,\varepsilon,n}(\Lambda)
respectively.

The classification of the indecomposable
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules was accomplished in
[
Sut94
]
(see also
[
GSTF06
]
) with two differences:
(1)
in
[
Sut94
]
the parameter
q
is a root of unity of even order and
(2)
in
[
Sut94
]
the algebra considered has generators
E,F,k^{\pm 1}
instead of
E,F,k^{\pm 1/2}
. In the rest of the appendix, we adapt the arguments in
[
Sut94
]
to our setting in order to prove the following

Proposition 4.18
.

(1)

The indecomposable
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules are the ones isomorphic to the modules
S_{n},P_{n}
,
\Omega^{\pm k}_{n},M^{k}_{n}(\Lambda)
and these modules are pairwise inequivalent.

(2)

The irreducible
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules are the ones isomorphic to the modules
S_{n}
.

(3)

The projective
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules are the ones isomorphic to the modules
S_{N-1},P_{n}
.

The proof of Proposition
4.18
is a straightforward adaptation of the arguments in
[
Sut94
]
so we reproduce them briefly and refer the reader to
[
Sut94
]
for more details. The first step is to decompose
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
into a direct sum of indecomposable left ideals. Write
P_{N-1}:=S_{N-1}
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
, set

\varphi_{n}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}}q^{-ni}k^{i/2}

so
k^{1/2}\varphi_{n}=\varphi_{n}k^{1/2}=q^{n}\varphi_{n}
,
E\varphi_{n}=\varphi_{n+1}E
and
F\varphi_{n}=\varphi_{n-1}F
. An easy application of the diamond Lemma for PBW bases shows that
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
admits the following basis

\{F^{a}\varphi_{n}E^{b},0\leq a,b,n\leq N-1\}.

In particular, if
x\in\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
is such that
Fx=0
then there exists a unique
y\in\operatorname{Span}(F^{a}\varphi_{n}E^{b},a\leq N-2)
such that
Fy=x
. For
0\leq n\leq N-2
, applying this remark to
x_{n}:=E^{\overline{n}}F^{N-1}\varphi_{n}
we obtain an element
\gamma_{n}\in\operatorname{Span}(F^{a}\varphi_{n}E^{b},a\leq N-2)
uniquely characterized by the fact that
F\gamma_{n}=x_{n}
. Set
\gamma_{N-1}:=F^{N-1}\varphi_{N-1}
and for
0\leq n\leq N-1
set

P_{n}(0):=\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\cdot\gamma_{n},\quad P_{n}(h)=P_{n}(0)\cdot E^{h}\quad h\in\{0,\ldots,n\}.

Lemma 4.19
.

The algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
admits the direct sum decomposition into left ideals

\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}=\oplus_{n=0}^{N-1}\oplus_{h=0}^{n}P_{n}(h)

where
P_{n}(h)
is isomorphic to
P_{n}
as a left module. Moreover the indecomposable projective
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
are the ones isomorphic to
P_{n}
.

Proof.

Clearly the
P_{n}(h)
are left ideals by definition and have trivial pairwise intersection. The fact that their direct sum is the whole
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
follows by dimension counting. The fact that
P_{n}(h)
is isomorphic to
P_{n}
is a straightforward verification left to the reader. Since the
P_{n}
are summands of the free rank
1

\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
module, they are projective. Conversely, we easily see that the tensor product
P_{a}\otimes P_{b}
decomposes as a direct sum of modules
P_{n}
, so any indecomposable summand of a free
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
module is isomorphic to a
P_{n}
.
∎

Set

\mathbb{B}_{N-1}:=\oplus_{h=0}^{N-1}P_{N-1}(h),\quad\mathbb{B}_{n}:=\left(\oplus_{h=0}^{n}P_{n}(h)\right)\oplus\left(\oplus_{h^{\prime}=0}^{\overline{n}}P_{\overline{n}}(h^{\prime})\right).

We obtain a decomposition

\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}=\mathbb{B}_{N-1}\oplus\oplus_{n=0}^{(N-3)/2}\mathbb{B}_{n}

where the
\mathbb{B}_{n}
are bilateral ideals. So classifying the indecomposable representations of
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
amount to classifying the indecomposable representations of each
\mathbb{B}_{n}
. Since
\mathbb{B}_{N-1}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, it admits a unique indecomposable representation which corresponds to
S_{N-1}=P_{N-1}
. Fix
0\leq n\leq(N-3)/2
and consider the algebra

\mathcal{B}:=\operatorname{End}_{\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(P_{n}\oplus P_{\overline{n}}).

By Morita theory, the functor
(P_{n}\oplus P_{\overline{n}})\otimes_{\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\bullet:\mathcal{B}^{op}-\operatorname{Mod}\to\mathbb{B}_{n}-\operatorname{Mod}
is an equivalence with quasi inverse
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}(P_{n}\otimes P_{\overline{n}},\mathbb{B}_{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\bullet
. Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.12
that
\operatorname{End}_{\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(P_{n})=\mathbb{C}id_{P_{n}}\oplus\mathbb{C}\pi_{n}
where
\pi_{n}^{2}=0
. We easily see that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(P_{n},P_{\overline{n}})=\mathbb{C}\alpha_{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}\beta_{n}
where
\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}
are characterized by the fact that they send the cyclic vector
y_{0}\in P_{n}
to
\alpha_{n}(y_{0})=x_{0}\in P_{\overline{n}}
and
\beta_{n}(y_{0})=y_{n+1}\in P_{\overline{n}}
. Note that
\alpha_{\overline{n}}\beta_{n}=\beta_{\overline{n}}\alpha_{n}=\pi_{n}
,
so
\mathcal{B}
is generated by the morphisms
id_{P_{n}},id_{P_{\overline{n}}},\alpha_{n},\alpha_{\overline{n}},\beta_{n}
and
\beta_{\overline{n}}
. Consider the Kronecker algebra
\mathcal{K}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X,Y]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X^{2},Y^{2})$}\right.}
and the embedding
j:\mathcal{B}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})
defined by

j(id_{P_{n}})=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},j(id_{P_{\overline{n}}})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},j(\alpha_{n})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
X&0\end{pmatrix},j_{\beta_{n}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
Y&0\end{pmatrix},j(\alpha_{\overline{n}})=\begin{pmatrix}0&X\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},j(\beta_{\overline{n}})=\begin{pmatrix}0&Y\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

The transposition
{}^{t}:\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})\to\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{op}
restricts to an isomorphism
t:\mathcal{B}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{B}^{op}
. Consider the
\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})-\mathcal{K}
bimodule
\mathcal{K}^{\oplus 2}
. The functor
(\mathcal{K}^{\oplus 2})\otimes_{\mathcal{K}}\bullet:\mathcal{K}-\operatorname{Mod}\to\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})-\operatorname{Mod}
is clearly an equivalence.

Consider the involutive automorphism
\Theta:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
defined by the conjugacy by
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})
, said differently
\Theta(id_{P_{n}})=id_{P_{\overline{n}}}
,
\Theta(\alpha_{n})=\alpha_{\overline{n}}
and
\Theta(\beta_{n})=\beta_{\overline{n}}
. For
L
a
\mathcal{B}
-module with module morphism
\rho:\mathcal{B}\to\operatorname{End}(L)
, let
\overline{L}
be the
\mathcal{B}
-module with module structure
\overline{\rho}(x):=\rho(\Theta(x))
,
x\in\mathcal{B}
. Consider the two projectors
P:=\frac{1+\Theta}{2}
,
Q:=\frac{1-\Theta}{2}
in
\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})
so that
P+Q=id
and
PQ=0
. Given
L\in\operatorname{Indecomp}(\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K}))
, we decompose it as
L=L^{+}\oplus L^{-}
where
L^{+}:=PL
and
L^{-}:=QL
are two indecomposable
\mathcal{B}
modules such that
L^{-}=\overline{L^{+}}
. We denote by
(\cdot)_{+},(\cdot)_{-}:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K}))\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{B})
sending
L
to
L_{+}
and
L_{-}
respectively. We can now define two maps
F_{+},F_{-}:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
as the compositions:

F_{+,-}:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})\xrightarrow[\cong]{(\mathcal{K}^{\oplus 2})\otimes_{\mathcal{K}}\bullet}\operatorname{Indecomp}(\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K}))\\
\xrightarrow{(\cdot)_{+,-}}\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{B})\xrightarrow[\cong]{t^{*}}\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{B}^{op})\xrightarrow[\cong]{(P_{n}\otimes P_{\overline{n}})\otimes\bullet}\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbb{B}_{n}),

and a map

F:=F_{+}\sqcup F_{-}:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})\sqcup\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbb{B}_{n}).

Lemma 4.20
.

F:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})\sqcup\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
is a bijection.

Proof.

We need to prove that
(\cdot)_{+}\sqcup(\cdot)_{-}:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K}))\sqcup\operatorname{Indecomp}(\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K}))\to\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{B})
is a bijection or, equivalently, that the map
(\cdot)_{+,-}:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K}))\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{B})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(M\sim\overline{M})$}\right.}
induced by both
(\cdot)_{+}
and
(\cdot)_{-}
, is a bijection. First, note that
\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})=\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}\theta
where
\theta=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
.
Let
M\in\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{B})
with module structure
\rho:\mathcal{B}\to\operatorname{End}(M)
and consider the
\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})
representation
\widehat{\rho}:\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K})\to\operatorname{End}(\widehat{M})
where
\widehat{M}=M\oplus\overline{M}
and

\overline{\rho}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\rho(x)&0\\
0&\overline{\rho}(x)\end{pmatrix},\quad\overline{\rho}(x\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\rho(x)\\
\overline{\rho}(x)&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mbox{ for }x\in\mathcal{B}.

Then
\widehat{M}\in\operatorname{Indecomp}(\operatorname{Mat}_{2}(\mathcal{K}))
and
(\widehat{M})_{+}=M
,
(\widehat{M})_{-}=\overline{M}
so the application
M\mapsto\widehat{M}
is the inverse of
(\cdot)_{+,-}
which is thus a bijection.

∎

It remains to prove that
\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})
is in bijection with
\Delta
. This classification was accomplished by Kronecker in
[
Kro31
]
and is easier to state using quiver representations in the framework of
[
Kac80
]
. First, note that if
\rho:\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
is an indecomposable representation with
\rho(XY)\neq 0
, then there exists
v\in V
with
\rho(XY)v\neq 0
and the subspace
\operatorname{Span}(v,\rho(X)v,\rho(Y)v,\rho(XY)v)\subset V
is a
\mathcal{K}
submodule isomorphic to
\mathcal{K}
(seen as left module over itself). Since this module is free, it is projective and thus
V\cong\mathcal{K}
.
In order to make the two modules
F_{+}(\mathcal{K})
and
F_{-}(\mathcal{K})
explicit, note that by Lemma
4.16
, the projection map
P_{n}\to S_{n}
given by Lemma
4.10
(sending
y_{0}
to
e_{0}
) is a projective cover, so

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{n},S_{\overline{n}})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(P_{n},P_{\overline{n}})=\mathbb{C}\alpha_{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}\beta_{n}.

The extension of
S_{n}
by
S_{\overline{n}}
corresponding to
z_{1}\alpha_{n}+z_{2}\beta_{n}
is the module
M_{n}(z_{1},z_{2})
with basis
(v_{i},i=0,\ldots N-1)
and module structure:

\displaystyle k^{1/2}v_{i}=A^{n-2i}v_{i},Fv_{N-1}=0,Fv_{n}=z_{2}v_{n+1},Fv_{i}=v_{i+1}\mbox{ for }i\neq n,N-1,

\displaystyle Ev_{0}=z_{1}v_{N-1},Ev_{i}=[i][n-i+1]v_{i-1}\mbox{ for }i\neq 0.

The extension is given by the exact sequence

0\to S_{\overline{n}}\xrightarrow{i}M_{n}(z_{1},z_{2})\xrightarrow{p}S_{n}\to 0

where
i(e_{j})=v_{n+j+1}
,
p(v_{i})=e_{i}
if
i\leq n
and
p(v_{i})=0
else. Note that
M_{n}(0,0)=S_{\overline{n}}\oplus S_{n}
and that, for
(z_{1},z_{2})\neq(0,0)
, then
M_{n}(z_{1},z_{2})\cong M_{n}^{1}(\lambda)
where
\lambda=z_{1}:z_{2}\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}
.
We denote by
S_{n}\xrightarrow{z_{1}\alpha_{n}+z_{2}\beta_{n}}S_{\overline{n}}
the extension
M_{n}(z_{1},z_{2})
. Then
\mathcal{K}
, seen as a
\mathcal{K}
module over itself, decomposes as

\mathcal{K}=\hbox to159.7pt{\vbox to78.46pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 79.84712pt\lower-39.2292pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-30.3776pt}{-13.72363pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -17.21 -10.15 C -5.35 -16.99 6.04 -16.99 17.42 -10.42}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.86603}{0.5}{-0.5}{0.86603}{12.76094pt}{-7.43193pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.96292pt}{-8.68938pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
and let
\mathbb{C}Q
be its path algebra; it is generated by the constant paths
1_{a},1_{b}
and the two paths
X,Y
. Consider the embedding
i:{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{K}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(XY)$}\right.}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}Q
defined by
i(X)=X
,
i(Y)=Y
and
i(1)=1_{a}+1_{b}
. Recall that a representation
(\rho,V)
of
Q
is the data of two vector spaces
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
and two linear morphisms
\rho(X),\rho(Y):V_{a}\to V_{b}
depicted as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(Y)}
and that such a representation induces a
\mathbb{C}Q
-module structure on
V_{a}\oplus V_{b}
in the obvious way such that one obtains an equivalence
\mathbb{C}Q-\operatorname{Mod}\cong\operatorname{Rep}(Q)
(see
[
Kac80
]
for details). The set of positive roots of
Q

is
\Delta_{+}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})=\{(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2},(n-m)^{2}\leq 1\}
, the real positive roots are the
(n,m)
for which
(n-m)^{2}=1
and the imaginary positive roots are the
(n,n),n\geq 1
. If
(\rho,V)
is an indecomposable representation of
Q
, then the pair
(\dim(V_{a}),\dim(V_{b}))
is a positive root and every positive roots (distinct from
(0,0)
) arise that way (see
[
Kac80
]
). To each real positive root corresponds exactly one indecomposable representation and each imaginary positive root corresponds to several indecomposable representations: for the quiver
Q
, one has a moduli space of such representations parametrized by
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
. Let us now describe the indecomposable representations of
\mathcal{K}
, and thus of
\mathbb{B}_{n}
.

(1)
The real roots
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
correspond to the representations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
both induces the trivial representation
\mathbb{C}
of
\mathcal{K}
which itself induces the modules
F_{+}(\mathbb{C})=S_{n}
and
F_{-}(\mathbb{C})=S_{\overline{n}}
.

(2)
For
k\geq 1
, the real positive root
(k+1,k)
corresponds to the representation

if
