which is
M_{n}^{k}(\lambda)
. Similarly, the image by
F_{-}
is
M_{\overline{n}}^{k}(\lambda)
.

Proof of Proposition
4.18
.

The classification of indecomposable
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules follows from Lemma
4.20
and the above classification of
\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathcal{K})
. The fact that the
P_{n}
are the unique projective indecomposable was proved in Lemma
4.16
. For
0\leq n\leq N-2
, then the modules
P_{n}
,
\Omega^{-k}_{n}
,
\Omega^{k}_{n}
and
M_{n}^{k}(\lambda)
admit the proper submodules
S_{\overline{n}}
,
S_{\overline{n}}^{\oplus k+1}
,
S_{\overline{n}}^{\oplus k}
and
S_{\overline{n}}^{\oplus k}
respectively, so they are not simple and the
S_{n}
are the unique simple
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

Theorem
4.6
is a consequence of Lemmas
4.14
,
4.16
and Proposition
4.18
.
∎

5.
Reduced stated skein algebras of 2P-marked surfaces and quantum cluster algebras

A
2P-marked surface
is a connected marked surface
\mathbf{\Sigma}
which has at least two boundary edges and no inner puncture (i.e. every boundary component contains at least one boundary edge). As we shall see, the reduced stated skein algebra
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
of a 2P-marked surface is isomorphic to a quantum cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
whose Azumaya loci have been studied in
[
MNTY22
]
. We will prove that these algebras are Azumaya. To relate these two algebras, we first introduce the
localized Müller algebra

\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
following
[
M1̈6
]
and define two isomorphisms
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\cong\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\cong\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

5.1.
Müller algebras and their localizations

During this subsection and the next one, we denote by
k
an arbitrary commutative unital ring and
A^{1/2}\in k^{\times}
an invertible element (not necessarily a root of unity).

Definition 5.1

(Müller skein algebras)
.

(
[
M1̈6
]
) Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be an essential marked surface.

(1)

The
Müller algebra

\mathcal{M}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the quotient of the
k
-free module generated by isotopy classes of (non stated) tangles
T\subset\mathbf{\Sigma}\times(0,1)

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 5.91 L -13.78 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 12.8 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.24706pt}{4.26794pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 12.8 -5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{9.24706pt}{-4.26794pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The multiplication in
\mathcal{M}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is defined by stacking the tangles as in Definition
2.3
.

(2)

For
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}
, let
\alpha(p)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
be the class of the corner arc. The
localized Müller algebra

\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}):=\mathcal{M}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})[\alpha_{p}^{-1},p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}]
is the algebra obtained from
\mathcal{M}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
by formally inverting the elements
\alpha(p)
.

A diagram
D
defines an element
[D]\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is spanned by elements of the form
[D]\alpha(p_{1})^{-k_{1}}\ldots\alpha(p_{n})^{-k_{n}}
where
\mathcal{P}^{\partial}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
and
k_{i}\geq 0
. An element
[D]\alpha(p_{1})^{-k_{1}}\ldots\alpha(p_{n})^{-k_{n}}
is called
reduced
if for each
p_{i}\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}
, either
k_{i}=0
or
D
does not contain any component isotopic to
\alpha(p_{i})
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{B}}^{M}\subset\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
be the subset of reduced elements
[D]\alpha(p_{1})^{-k_{1}}\ldots\alpha(p_{n})^{-k_{n}}
such that
D
is simple.

Proposition 5.3
.

(
[
M1̈6
, Proposition
5.3
]
)
\underline{\mathcal{B}}^{M}
is a basis of
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

As remarked in
[
Le18
]
, by comparing the skein relations in Definitions
2.3
and
5.1
, we see that there is an algebra morphism
f:\mathcal{M}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
sending the class
[T]
of a tangle to
f([T]):=[T,s^{+}]
where
s^{+}
sends every points of
\partial T
to
+
. Since
\alpha(p)_{++}
has inverse
\alpha(p)_{--}
,
f
extends to a morphism (denoted by the same letter)
f:\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
sending
\alpha(p)^{-1}
to
\alpha(p)_{--}
.

Theorem 5.4
.

(
[
LYa
, Theorem
5.2
]
)
f:\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is an isomorphism.

Remark 5.5
.

By comparing Definitions
5.2
and
2.12
and using Lemma
2.15
, we see that for each
b\in\underline{\mathcal{B}}^{M}
there exists
n_{b}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
f
sends the basis
\{A^{n_{b}/2}b,b\in\underline{\mathcal{B}}^{M}\}
to the basis
\mathcal{B}^{M}

so the theorem follows from Propositions
2.13
and
5.3
.

5.2.
Quantum cluster algebras

Quantum cluster algebras were introduced in
[
BZ05
]
after the introduction of classical cluster algebras in
[
FZ02
]
and their Poisson structure in
[
GSV03
]
. We will closely follow the notations in
[
M1̈6
]
.

Definition 5.6

(Quantum cluster algebras and their upper algebras)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a skew field with
k\subset\mathcal{F}
and such that
\mathcal{F}
is purely transcendental over
k
.

(1)

A
quantum seed
in
\mathcal{F}
is a triple
\mathbb{S}=(B,\Lambda,M)
where:

•

the
exchange matrix

B
is an
N\times ex

\mathbb{Z}
-valued matrix where
ex\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that, if
\pi:\mathbb{Z}^{N}\to\mathbb{Z}^{ex}
denotes the projection, then
\pi B
is skew-symmetric;

•

the
compatibility matrix

\Lambda
is an
N\times N
skew symmetric matrix valued in
\mathbb{Z}
such that
\Lambda B=D\iota
where
D
is diagonal matrix with positive diagonal elements and
\iota
is the matrix of the inclusion
\mathbb{Z}^{ex}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{N}
;

•

M:\mathbb{Z}^{N}\to\mathcal{F}\setminus\{0\}
is a map such that
M(\alpha)M(\beta)=A^{\frac{1}{2}\Lambda(\alpha,\beta)}M(\alpha+\beta)
and such that
\mathcal{F}
is the field of fractions of the quantum torus
kM(\mathbb{Z}^{N})
.

(2)

For
\mathbb{S}=(B,\Lambda,M)
a quantum seed and
i\in ex
, the
mutation of
\mathbb{S}

in the direction of
i
is the quantum seed
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=(B^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime})
characterized by the following properties:

•

B^{\prime}_{jk}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-B_{jk}&\mbox{, if }i=j\mbox{ or }i=k;\\
B_{jk}+\frac{1}{2}(|B_{ji}|B_{ik}+B_{ji}|B_{ik}|)&\mbox{, else.}\end{array}\right.
;

•

for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
such that
\alpha_{i}=0
then
M(\alpha)=M^{\prime}(\alpha)
;

•

one has

M^{\prime}(e_{i})=M\left(-e_{i}+\sum_{B_{ji}>0}B_{ji}e_{j}\right)+M\left(-e_{i}-\sum_{B_{ji}<0}B_{ji}e_{j}\right).

By
[
BZ05
, Section
4.4
]
,
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
exists and is unique. We denote by
\operatorname{Mut}(\mathbb{S})
the set of quantum seeds which can be obtained from
\mathbb{S}
by a finite sequence of mutations.

(3)

The
quantum cluster algebra

\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S})
is the
k
subalgebra of
\mathcal{F}
generated by elements
M^{\prime}(\alpha)
for
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=(B^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Mut}(\mathbb{S})
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
such that
\alpha_{i}\geq 0
for
i\in ex
.

(4)

The
quantum upper cluster algebra

\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathbb{S})
is the intersection:

\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathbb{S}):=\bigcap_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=(B^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Mut}(\mathbb{S})}kM^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}^{N}).

By
[
BZ05
, Corollary
5.2
]
, one has
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S})\subset\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathbb{S})
(this is the so-called Laurent phenomenon). In many cases, this inclusion is an equality. One can associate to any triangulated marked surface
(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
a quantum seed. This was first remarked at the classical level in
[
GSV05
]
and extended to the quantum case in
[
M1̈6
]
.

Definition 5.7

(Seeds associated to triangulated marked surfaces)
.

(1)

A connected marked surface
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is
triangulable
if it is essential, has no inner puncture and is not a disc with one or two boundary edges. For such a
\mathbf{\Sigma}
, an
\mathcal{A}
-
triangulation

\Delta
is a maximal set of pairwise disjoint and pairwise non isotopic arcs with an indexation
\Delta=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{|\Delta|}\}
(recall from Definition
2.2
than an arc is an open connected diagram without crossing). Clearly every corner arc
\alpha(p)
belongs to
\Delta
and we denote by
\mathring{\Delta}\subset\Delta
the set of arcs which are not corner arcs and by
ex\subset\{1,\ldots,|\Delta|\}
the subset of indices
i
such that
x_{i}\in\mathring{\Delta}
. For
x_{i}\in\Delta
, we denote by
\partial_{1}x_{i}
and
\partial_{2}x_{i}
its two endpoints (indexed arbitrarily). For two endpoints
\partial_{i}x
and
\partial_{j}y
which belong to the same boundary edge
a
(where
x,y\in\Delta
,
i,j\in\{1,2\}
) such that
\partial_{i}x<_{\mathfrak{o}^{+}}\partial_{j}y
, we say that
\partial_{i}x
and
\partial_{j}y
are
consecutive
if there does not exist
z\in\Delta
and
k\in\{1,2\}
such that
\partial_{i}x<_{\mathfrak{o}^{+}}\partial_{k}z<_{\mathfrak{o}^{+}}\partial_{j}y
.

(2)

Let
(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
be a triangulated marked surface and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
the field of fraction of
\mathcal{M}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
(obtained by inverting every non zero elements). Let
\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)}=(B^{\Delta},\Lambda^{\Delta},M^{\Delta})
be the quantum seed over
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
defined by:

•

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\Delta|\}
and
j\in ex
,

B^{\Delta}_{i,j}:=\sum_{u,v\in\{1,2\}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-1&\mbox{, if }\partial_{u}x_{i}\mbox{ and }\partial_{v}x_{j}\mbox{ are in the same boundary edge }\mbox{ and }\partial_{u}x_{i}<_{\mathfrak{o}^{+}}\partial_{v}x_{j}\mbox{ are consecutive};\\
+1&\mbox{, if }\partial_{u}x_{i}\mbox{ and }\partial_{v}x_{j}\mbox{ are in the same boundary edge }\mbox{ and }\partial_{v}x_{j}<_{\mathfrak{o}^{+}}\partial_{u}x_{i}\mbox{ are consecutive};\\
0&\mbox{, if }\partial_{u}x_{i}\mbox{ and }\partial_{v}x_{j}\mbox{ else.}\end{array}\right.

•

For
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,|\Delta|\}
,

\Lambda^{\Delta}_{i,j}:=\sum_{u,v\in\{1,2\}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{, if }\partial_{u}x_{i}\mbox{ and }\partial_{v}x_{j}\mbox{ are in two distinct boundary edges;}\\
+1&\mbox{, if }\partial_{u}x_{i}\mbox{ and }\partial_{v}x_{j}\mbox{ are in the same boundary edge }\mbox{ and }\partial_{u}x_{i}<_{\mathfrak{o}^{+}}\partial_{v}x_{j};\\
-1&\mbox{, if }\partial_{u}x_{i}\mbox{ and }\partial_{v}x_{j}\mbox{ are in the same boundary edge }\mbox{ and }\partial_{v}x_{j}<_{\mathfrak{o}^{+}}\partial_{u}x_{i}.\end{array}\right.

•

M^{\Delta}(e_{i}):=x_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\Delta|\}
.

By
[
M1̈6
, Proposition
7.8
]
,
\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)}
is indeed a quantum seed.

(3)

By
[
M1̈6
, Theorem
7.9
]
, one has
\operatorname{Mut}(\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)})=\{\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta^{\prime})},\Delta^{\prime}\mbox{ an }\mathcal{A}-\mbox{ triangulation of }\mathbf{\Sigma}\}
. Therefore
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)})
and
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)})
only depend on
\mathbf{\Sigma}
and not on
\Delta
; we denote them by
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and
\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
respectively.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
be a triangulated marked surface.

(1)

[
M1̈6
, Theorem
7.16
]
We have the inclusions
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\subset\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\subset\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

(2)

[
M1̈6
, Theorem
9.7
]
If moreover
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is a 2P-marked surface, then
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\mathcal{M}^{0}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Therefore, for a 2P-marked surface
\mathbf{\Sigma}
which is not the bigon, using Theorems
5.4
and
5.8
, we have an isomorphism
f:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

5.3.
Representations of reduced stated skein algebras of 2P-surfaces

For now on, we suppose that
k=\mathbb{C}
and that
A^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
is a root of unity of odd order
N\geq 3
. So
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S})
is now a complex algebra.

Definition 5.9

(Frobenius morphisms of quantum cluster algebras)
.

Suppose that the field
\mathcal{F}
is purely transcendental over
\mathbb{C}
and let
\mathbb{S}=(B,\Lambda,M)
a quantum seed for
\mathcal{F}
. Let
Z_{\mathbb{S}}
denote the center of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S})
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{+1}(\mathbb{S})
be the classical cluster algebra where we set
A^{1/2}=+1
.

(1)

The
Frobenius morphism

Fr_{\mathbb{S}}:\mathcal{A}_{+1}(\mathbb{S})\hookrightarrow Z_{\mathbb{S}}
is the embedding defined by
Fr_{\mathbb{S}}(M(\alpha))=M(N\alpha)
.

(2)

We write
\widehat{X}(\mathbb{S}):=\operatorname{MaxSpec}(Z_{\mathbb{S}})
and
X(\mathbb{S}):=\operatorname{MaxSpec}(\mathcal{A}_{+1}(\mathbb{S}))
and denote by
\pi:\widehat{X}(\mathbb{S})\to X(\mathbb{S})
the dominant map defined by
Fr_{\mathbb{S}}
.

Note that

Fr_{\mathbb{S}}(M(\alpha))M(\beta)=M(N\alpha)M(\beta)=(A^{1/2})^{N}M(N\alpha+\beta)=M(\beta)=M(\beta+N\alpha)=M(\beta)Fr_{\mathbb{S}}(M(\alpha))

so the image of the Frobenius is indeed central.
Since
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S})\subset\mathcal{F}
is a subalgebra of a field, it is a domain. It is affine and is finitely generated over the image of
Fr_{\mathbb{S}}
so it is almost Azumaya. We denote by

\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{S})\subset\widehat{X}(\mathbb{S})
its Azumaya locus and by
\mathcal{FAL}(\mathbb{S})\subset X(\mathbb{S})
its fully Azumaya locus (i.e. the set of
x\in X(\mathbb{S})
such that
\pi^{-1}(x)\subset\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{S})
).

Theorem 5.10
.

(
[
MNTY22
, Theorem
6.1
]
) Suppose that the skew field
\mathcal{F}
is a purely transcendental over
\mathbb{C}
and let
\mathbb{S}=(B,\Lambda,M)
a quantum seed for
\mathcal{F}
.
Suppose that

(1)

N
is prime to the diagonal entries of the diagonal matrix
D
defined by
\Lambda B=D\iota
(recall that
\iota
is the matrix of the inclusion
\mathbb{Z}^{ex}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{N}
) and

(2)

one has
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S})=\mathcal{U}_{q}(\mathbb{S})
.

Then the fully Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S})
contains the regular locus of
X(\mathbb{S})
. In particular, if
X(\mathbb{S})
is smooth, then
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbb{S})
is Azumaya.

Theorem
5.10
permits to prove Theorem
1.2
for 2P-marked surfaces. We will also use the following:

Theorem 5.11
.

(
[
Kor22
, Theorem
1
]
) If
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma_{g,1},\{a\})
is a connected marked surface with a single boundary component which has a single boundary edge, then
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is Azumaya.

Corollary 5.12
.

For
\mathbf{\Sigma}
a connected essential marked surface without inner puncture, then
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is Azumaya and every semi-weight representation is a weight representation.

Proof.

Let us prove that
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is Azumaya. The second assertion will then follow from Theorem
2.30
.
If
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbb{B}
is the bigon (a disc with two boundary edges), then
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbb{B})\cong\mathbb{C}[X^{\pm 1}]
is commutative, so the result is obvious.
If
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma_{g,1},\{a\})
has a single boundary component which has a single boundary edge, it is Theorem
5.11
.
Else,
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is a triangulable 2P marked surface; let
\Delta
be an
\mathcal{A}
-triangulation. The isomorphism
f:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{A}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
induces an isomorphism
X(\mathbf{\Sigma})\cong X(\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)})
so Theorem
3.5
implies that
X(\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)})
is smooth and it suffices to prove that the two hypotheses of Theorem
5.10
are satisfied for
\mathbb{S}_{(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)}
. In the proof of
[
M1̈6
, Proposition
7.8
]
, it is proved that
\Lambda^{\Delta}B^{\Delta}=4\iota
and since
N
is odd, the first hypothesis is satisfied. The second follows from Theorem
5.8
. Therefore Theorem
5.10
implies that
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\cong\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is Azumaya.
∎

6.
Gluing marked surfaces together

If
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are modules over
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
respectively and
a_{1},a_{2}
some boundary edges of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}
respectively, then
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
is a
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}\cup_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
-module by precomposing with
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
.
The goal of this section is to prove the

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}
be marked surfaces and for
i=1,2
let
a_{i}
be a boundary edges of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i}
such that the connected component of
\partial\Sigma_{i}
which contains
a_{i}
also contains at least another boundary edge.
Write
\mathbf{\Sigma}:=\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}\cup_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}
.
Then

\widehat{p}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}\left(\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})\right)=\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma}).

Moreover, any indecomposable (semi) weight
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
-module is isomorphic to a module
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
with
V_{i}
a (semi) weight indecomposable
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
module. Conversely, any such
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
module
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
is indecomposable of the same kind (weight or semi weight).

Let
Z^{1}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
be the subalgebra of
Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
generated by
Z^{0}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
and the elements
\alpha_{\partial}^{\pm 1}
for
\partial\in\Gamma^{\partial}
; thus
Z^{0}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\subset Z^{1}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\subset Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
6.1
, any element of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\otimes\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
is the product of an element of the image of
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
with an element of
Z^{1}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}}\otimes Z^{1}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}}
.

Proof.

Let us introduce some notations illustrated in Figure
9

(i)
.
Let
\partial_{1}
and
\partial_{2}
be the boundary components of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
containing
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
. The orientations of
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
induces orientations of
\partial_{1}
and
\partial_{2}
thus a cyclic order on their sets of boundary edges and an orientation of each boundary edge. Let
p_{1},p_{1}^{\prime}
be the two punctures adjacent to
a_{1}
such that
a_{1}
is oriented from
p_{1}^{\prime}
to
p_{1}
. Let
p_{2},p_{2}^{\prime}
be the two punctures adjacent to
a_{2}
such that
a_{2}
is oriented from
p_{2}
to
p_{2}^{\prime}
. Write
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}\cup_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}
. Let
d\subset\Sigma
be the common image of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
in the quotient map
\pi:\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}\to\Sigma
and write
p:=\pi(p_{1})=\pi(p_{2})
and
p^{\prime}:=\pi(p_{1}^{\prime})=\pi(p_{2}^{\prime})
. Let
b_{1},c_{1}
be the boundary edges in
\partial_{1}
such that
p_{1}
is adjacent to
a_{1}
and
c_{1}
and
p_{2}
is adjacent to
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
(if
\partial_{1}
only has two boundary edges, then
c_{1}=b_{1}
). Let
b_{2},c_{2}
be the boundary edges in
\partial_{2}
such that
p_{2}
is adjacent to
a_{2}
and
c_{2}
and
p_{2}^{\prime}
is adjacent to
b_{2}
and
a_{2}
. Let
R\subset\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\otimes\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
be the subalgebra of elements which are a product of an element of
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\right)
with an element of

Z^{1}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}}\otimes Z^{1}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}}
.
We need to show that for every
[D_{1},s_{1}]\in\mathcal{B}^{M}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})
and for every
[D_{2},s_{2}]\in\mathcal{B}^{M}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
then
[D_{1},s_{1}]\otimes 1\in R
and
1\otimes[D_{2},s_{2}]\in R
.
Let us first prove the following:

Claim:
For
\varepsilon=\pm
, then the four elements
\alpha(p_{1})_{\varepsilon\varepsilon}\otimes 1
,
\alpha(p^{\prime}_{1})_{\varepsilon\varepsilon}\otimes 1
,
1\otimes\alpha(p_{2})_{\varepsilon\varepsilon}
and
1\otimes\alpha(p^{\prime}_{2})_{\varepsilon\varepsilon}
are in
R
.

We prove the claim for
\alpha(p_{1})_{++}\otimes 1
and leave the other similar cases to the reader.

\bullet

\underline{\alpha(p_{1})_{--}\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})_{++}\otimes 1\in R:}
Choose two points
v\in b_{1}
,
w\in c_{1}
and consider an arc
\beta
oriented from
v
to
w
such that
\beta
can be isotoped relatively to its boundary to an arc
\beta^{\prime}\subset\partial_{1}
containing
a_{1}
. As illustrated in Figure
9

(ii)
, one has
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}(\beta_{+-})=A^{1/2}\alpha(p_{1})_{--}\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})_{++}\otimes 1\in R
.

\bullet

\underline{\alpha(p_{1})_{--}\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})_{--}\otimes 1\in R:}
Let
(p_{1}^{\prime},p_{1},p_{3},p_{4},\ldots,p_{n})
be the punctures of
\partial_{1}
cyclically ordered. Clearly, for
i=3,\ldots,n
,
\alpha(p_{i})_{--}\otimes 1
is in the image of
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
. Moreover
\alpha_{\partial_{1}}^{-1}=\alpha(p_{1})_{--}\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})_{--}\alpha(p_{3})_{--}\ldots\alpha(p_{n})_{--}\in Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}}
, therefore
\alpha(p_{1})_{--}\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})_{--}\otimes 1\in R
.

\bullet

\underline{\alpha(p_{1})_{++}^{N}\otimes 1\in R:}
By Lemma
2.15
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(\alpha(p_{1})_{++})^{N}=A^{n/2}\alpha(p_{1})_{++}^{(N)}
. Since
\alpha(p_{1})_{++}^{(N)}
is in the image of the Frobenius morphism by Theorem
2.23
, one has
(\alpha(p_{1})_{++})^{N}\otimes 1\in R
.

\bullet

\underline{\alpha(p_{1})_{++}\otimes 1\in R:}
First one has
(\alpha(p_{1})_{--})^{2}=(\alpha(p_{1})_{--}\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})_{++})(\alpha(p_{1})_{--}\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})_{--})\in R
. Write
N=2k+1
. Then
\alpha(p_{1})_{++}=(\alpha(p_{1})_{--})^{2k}(\alpha(p_{1})_{++})^{N}\in R.

The other cases of the claim are proved similarly. Let
[D,s]\in\mathcal{B}^{M}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
and let us prove that
1\otimes[D,s]\in R
. By Lemma
2.15
there exist
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
D^{0}\subset D
a sub diagram such that
[D,s]=A^{n/2}[D^{0},s^{+}]\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}}\alpha(p)_{--}^{n_{p}}
, where
s^{+}
is totally positive in the sense that it sends every endpoints in
\partial D_{0}
to
+
. Since every elements
1\otimes(\alpha(p)_{--})^{n_{p}}
are in
R
, it suffices to prove that
1\otimes[D^{0},s^{+}]\in R
for a totally positive simple stated diagram. For such a
D^{0}\subset\Sigma_{2}
, let
\widehat{D^{0}}\subset\Sigma
be the simple diagram obtained from
D^{0}
by pushing slightly each point of
\partial_{a_{2}}D^{0}
to
b_{1}
while forming a corner arc as in Figure
9

(iii)
. If
m:=|\partial_{a_{2}}D^{0}|
then as illustrated in Figure
9

(iii)
, one has

\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}([\widehat{D^{0}},s^{+}])=(\alpha(p^{\prime}_{1})_{++})^{m}\otimes[D^{0},s^{+}].

Since
\alpha(p^{\prime}_{1})_{++}\otimes 1\in R
by the claim, we obtain that
1\otimes[D^{0},s^{+}]\in R
. So every element of the form
1\otimes[D,s]
is in
R
. We prove that the elements
[D,s]\otimes 1
belong to
R
similarly. This concludes the proof.

Figure 9.
(i)
The marked surfaces
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2},\mathbf{\Sigma}
and some boundary edges;
(ii)
An illustration of the equality
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}(\beta_{+-})=A^{1/2}\alpha(p_{1})_{--}\alpha(p_{1}^{\prime})_{++}\otimes 1
;
(iii)
A diagram
(D^{0},s^{+})
, its associated diagram
(\widehat{D^{0}},s^{+})
and an illustration of the equality
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}([\widehat{D^{0}},s^{+}])=(\alpha(p^{\prime}_{1})_{++})^{m}\otimes[D^{0},s^{+}]
.

∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\rho:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
be an indecomposable semi-weight representation and
\partial\in\Gamma^{\partial}
. Then there exists
h_{\partial}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
\rho(\alpha_{\partial})=h_{\partial}id_{V}
.

Proof.

The operator
\rho(\alpha_{\partial})
is annihilated by the polynomial
P(X)=X^{N}-\chi_{x}(\alpha_{\partial})
so the minimal polynomial of
\rho(\alpha_{\partial})
divides
P(X)
. Since
\rho(\alpha_{\partial})
commutes with the image of
\rho
and since
\rho
is indecomposable, its minimal polynomial has the form
(X-c)^{n}
.
Since
P(X)
has its roots of multiplicity one, the minimal polynomial of
\rho(\alpha_{\partial})
has the form
X-h_{\partial}
for some
h_{\partial}
such that
h_{\partial}^{N}=\chi_{x}(\alpha_{\partial})
.
∎

Therefore, for
\rho:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
an indecomposable semi-weight representation, one can associate a tuple
\widetilde{x}=(x;h_{\partial})_{\partial\in\Gamma^{\partial}}\in\operatorname{MaxSpec}(Z^{1}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})=:\widetilde{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
which we call the middle shadow of
\rho
. Then
\rho
factorizes through the algebra

\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})_{\widetilde{x}}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\widetilde{x}\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})$}\right.}.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

The case of weight representations:

Let
\widehat{x}\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. By Theorem
3.10
, there exist
\widehat{x}_{i}\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i})
,
i=1,2
, such that
\widehat{p}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}(\widehat{x}_{1},\widehat{x}_{2})=\widehat{x}
. The splitting morphism
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
induces an injective morphism

\widetilde{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})_{\widehat{x}}\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})_{\widehat{x}_{1}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})_{\widehat{x}_{2}}.

By Lemma
6.2
,
\widetilde{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
is surjective. To prove that it is injective, recall that by definition,
\widetilde{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
is obtained from the injective morphism
{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
by passing to the quotient. So we need to prove the inclusion
{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{I}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\widehat{x}_{2}}\right)\subset\mathcal{I}_{\widehat{x}}
; this follows from the inclusion
{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}^{-1}\left(Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}}\otimes 1+1\otimes Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}}\right)\subset Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
which immediately follows from the description of the centers in Theorem
2.27
.

So
\widetilde{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
is an isomorphism. By definition, an indecomposable weight
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
module with full shadow
\widehat{x}
is the same than an indecomposable representation of the finite dimensional algebra
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})_{\widehat{x}}
(and similarly for
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i}
). Therefore there is a
1:1
correspondance between isomorphism classes of pairs of indecomposable weight representation
(V_{1},V_{2})
where
V_{i}
has full shadow
\widehat{x}_{i}
and the set of isomorphism classes of weight
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
representations with full shadow
\widehat{x}
; the isomorphism sends
(V_{1},V_{2})
to
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
. By Theorem
2.20
,
\widehat{x}\in\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
if and only if
\dim\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})_{\widehat{x}}\right)=(D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})^{2}
and if
\widehat{x}\notin\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
then
\dim\left(\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})_{\widehat{x}}\right)<(D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})^{2}
(and similarly for
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i}
). By Lemma
2.26
, we have
D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}}D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}}
so
\widehat{x}\in\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
if and only if
x_{i}\in\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i})
for
i=1,2
.

The case of semi weight representations:
Let
\widetilde{x}=(x,h_{\partial})_{\partial\in\Gamma^{\partial}}\in\widetilde{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and denote by
\widetilde{p}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}:\widetilde{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\widetilde{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})\to\widetilde{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
the morphism induced by
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
.
As showed in the proof of Theorem
3.10
, there exists
(\widetilde{x}_{1},\widetilde{x}_{2})\in\widetilde{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})\times\widetilde{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})
such that
\widetilde{p}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}(\widetilde{x}_{1},\widetilde{x}_{2})=\widetilde{x}
.
\theta_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
induces a morphism

\widetilde{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})_{\widetilde{x}}\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1})_{\widetilde{x}_{1}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2})_{\widetilde{x}_{2}}.

Again Lemma
6.2
proves the surjectivity of
\widetilde{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
and the injectivity follows from the inclusion

{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{I}_{\widetilde{x}_{1}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\widetilde{x}_{2}}\right)\subset\mathcal{I}_{\widetilde{x}}
, so
\widetilde{\theta}_{a_{1}\#a_{2}}
is an isomorphism. Therefore we have a
1:1
correspondance between indecomposable representations
V
of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
with shadow
\widetilde{x}
and pairs
(V_{1},V_{2})
with
V_{i}
an indecomposable
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i})
semi weight module with shadow
\widetilde{x}_{i}
(the correspondence sends
(V_{1},V_{2})
to
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
). This concludes the proof.
∎

7.
Classification of semi-weight representations of reduced stated skein algebras

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a connected essential marked surface.
For
x\in X(\mathbf{\Sigma})
, we denote by
\rho_{x}:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
the functor associated by Corollary
3.3
. Recall that
x

is central at

p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}
if
\rho_{x}(\gamma_{p})=\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
for
\gamma_{p}
a peripheral loop encircling
p
.

Let
\widehat{x}=(x,h_{p},h_{\partial})_{p,\partial}\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and decompose the set of inner punctures as
\mathring{\mathcal{P}}=\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{0}\sqcup\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\sqcup\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
where

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{2}:=\{p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}},\mbox{ such that }\rho_{x}(\gamma_{p})=\pm\mathds{1}_{2}\mbox{ and }h_{p}\neq\mp 2\}

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{1}:=\{p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}\setminus\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{2},\mbox{ such that }\operatorname{tr}(\rho_{x}(\gamma_{p}))=\pm 2\mbox{ and }h_{p}\neq\pm 2\}.

Definition 7.1

(Colorings)
.

A map
\sigma:\mathring{\mathcal{P}}\to\Delta\bigsqcup\overline{\Delta}
is called a
coloring compatible with

\widehat{x}
if
(1)
for
p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
, then
\sigma(p)=S
and
(2)
if
p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
, then
\sigma(p)\in\{S,P\}
. By convention, if
\mathring{\mathcal{P}}=\emptyset
, we consider that every
\widehat{x}
has a unique coloring. The set of colorings compatible with
\widehat{x}
will be denoted by
\operatorname{col}(\widehat{x})
and we write
m(\widehat{x}):=|\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{2}|
.

Let
\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
be the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable semi-weight
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
modules and let
\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\widehat{x})\subset\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
the subset of these modules with maximal shadow
\widehat{x}
.
Denote by
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
the category of semi weight
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
modules. The main theorem of this paper is the following:

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma,\mathcal{A})
be a connected essential marked surface which either has a boundary component with at least two boundary edges or which does not have any inner puncture.
Let
\widehat{x}=(x,h_{p},h_{\partial})\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

(1)

We have
\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\widehat{X}^{reg}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
, i.e. the Azumaya locus of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is equal to the regular locus of
\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. Moreover

\widehat{x}\in\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
if and only if
m=0
.

(2)

The fully Azumaya locus
\mathcal{FAL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the locus of representations which are not central at any inner puncture. For
x\in\mathcal{FAL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
the indecomposable semi-weight representations with classical shadow
x
are classified (up to isomorphism) by their full shadows, so their set is in
1:1
correspondance with the fiber
\widehat{\pi}^{-1}(x)\subset\widehat{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

(3)

The indecomposable semi-weight representations with maximal shadow
\widehat{x}
are in
1:1
correspondence with the set of colorings compatible with
\widehat{x}
so one has

\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\bigsqcup_{\widehat{x}\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\operatorname{Indecomp}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\widehat{x})\cong\bigsqcup_{\widehat{x}\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\operatorname{col}(\widehat{x}).

(4)

The indecomposable weight representations with full shadow
\widehat{x}
correspond to the colorings taking values in
\{S,\overline{S},((1,1),1),\overline{((1,1),1)}\}
: there are thus
4^{m}
such representations.

(5)

The irreducible representations with full shadow
\widehat{x}
correspond to the colorings taking values in
\{S,\overline{S}\}
: there are thus
2^{m}
such representations.

(6)

The indecomposable semi-weight representations with maximal shadow
\widehat{x}
which are projective objects in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
correspond to the colorings sending the elements of
\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
to
P
and the elements of
\mathring{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
to an element in
\{P,\overline{P}\}
: there are thus
2^{m}
such representations.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{\Sigma}
does not have inner puncture then the theorem follows from Corollary
5.12
. Else, it contains a boundary component with at least two boundary edges.
Write
\mathring{\mathcal{P}}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
.

\mathbf{\Sigma}
can be obtained from a 2P-marked surface
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}
and
n
copies of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
together, i.e.

\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}\cup_{a_{1}\#b_{1}}\mathbb{D}_{1}\cup_{a_{2}\#b_{2}}\ldots\cup_{a_{n}\#b_{n}}\mathbb{D}_{1}

for some boundary edges
a_{i},b_{i}
.
Let
\theta:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0})\otimes\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1})^{\otimes n}
be the induced splitting morphism and
\pi:\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0})\times\widehat{X}(\mathbb{D}_{1})^{\times n}\to\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
the morphism defined by the restriction of
\theta
to the center. By Theorem
6.1
, one has
\pi(\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0})\times\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})^{\times n})=\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. By Corollary
5.12
,
\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0})=\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0})
and by Corollary
4.8
,
\mathcal{AL}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is the set of elements
(g,h_{p},h_{\partial})
such that either
\varphi(g)\neq\pm\mathds{1}_{1}
or
\varphi(g)=\pm 2
and
h_{p}=\mp 2
. Therefore
\mathcal{AL}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the set of elements
\widehat{x}=(x,h_{p},h_{\partial})
such that for every
p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}
then either
\rho_{x}(\alpha_{p})\neq\pm\mathds{1}_{1}
or
\rho_{x}(\alpha_{p})=\pm\mathds{1}_{1}
and
h_{p}=\mp 2
. Together with Theorem
3.8
, this proves
(1)
.
Assertion
(2)
follows from Assertion
(1)
together with Theorem
2.30
.

Let
\widehat{x}\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. By Theorem
3.10
, there exists
(\widehat{x}_{0},\widehat{x}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{n})\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0})\times\widehat{X}(\mathbb{D}_{1})^{n}
such that
p(\widehat{x}_{0},\widehat{x}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{n})=\widehat{x}
.
By Theorem
6.1
the (semi) weight indecomposable
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
module with classical shadow
\widehat{x}
have the form

V_{0}\otimes V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}
where
V_{0}
is an indecomposable (semi) weight
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0})
module with shadow
\widehat{x}_{0}
and
V_{i}
is an indecomposable (semi) weight
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
-module with shadow
\widehat{x}_{i}
. By Corollary
5.12

V_{0}
is unique up to isomorphism.
For
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, write
\widehat{x}_{i}=(g_{i},h_{p_{i}},h_{\partial}^{i})
and let
I^{2}:=\{i\mbox{ such that }p_{i}\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}^{2}\}
and
I^{1}:=\{i\mbox{ such that }p_{i}\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}^{1}\}
.
By Corollary
4.8
, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we have up to isomorphism:
(i)
a single possible
V_{i}
if
i\notin I^{1}\cup I^{2}
,
(ii)
two possible semi-weight modules
V_{i}
if
i\in I^{1}
and one of them is a weight module,
(iii)

if
i\in I^{2}
, the indecomposable semi-weight modules
V_{i}
are in
1:1
correspondance with
\Delta\sqcup\overline{\Delta}
. Therefore, the isomorphism classes of semi weight
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
modules
with shadow
\widehat{x}
are in
1:1
correspondance with the set of colorings admissible at
\widehat{x}
and we have proved
(3)
. Such an indecomposable representation
V_{0}\otimes V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}
is weight/ simple/projective in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
if and only if each of the
V_{i}
has the same property so
(4)
,
(5)
,
(6)
follow from Corollary
4.8
.
This concludes the proof.

∎

8.
Representations of unreduced stated skein algebras and the unrestricted quantum moduli algebras

8.1.
Statement of the main result

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be an essential marked surface and denote by
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
the marked surface obtained from
\mathbf{\Sigma}
by gluing a triangle along each boundary edge of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
. So each boundary edge
a
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
corresponds to two boundary edges, say
a^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
. Let
i:\mathbf{\Sigma}\to\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
be the embedding which is the identity outside a small neighborhood of
\mathcal{A}
and embedding
a
into
a^{\prime}
. It is proved in
[
LYa
]
that the morphism
i_{*}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
induces an injective morphism

j:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}).

Therefore, for each (semi-weight) representation
r:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
, we can associate a representation
r^{*}=r\circ j:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
. Such a representation will be called a
reduced representation
. Note that since we assume that
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is essential, then each connected component of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
as a boundary component with at least two boundary edges. Thus Theorem
7.2
provides a classification of such reduced representations and thus a lot of examples. The goal of this subsection is to prove the

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{A})
be a connected essential marked surface of genus
g
with
n
boundary components such that each boundary component contains at most one boundary edge. So it has
k:=n-|\mathcal{A}|
inner punctures
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
and
|\mathcal{A}|
boundary punctures
p_{\partial_{1}},\ldots,p_{\partial_{|\mathcal{A}|}}
.

(1)

The center
\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is generated by the image
\underline{Z}^{0}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
of the Frobenius morphism together with the peripheral curves
\gamma_{p_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{p_{k}}
. So the closed points of
\widehat{\underline{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}):=\operatorname{MaxSpec}(\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})
are in
1:1
correspondence with the set of elements
\widehat{\rho}=(\rho,h_{p_{1}},\ldots,h_{p_{k}})
with
\rho:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
h_{p_{i}}\in\mathbb{C}
is such that
T_{N}(h_{p_{i}})=-\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))
.

(2)

The PI-degree
\underline{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is equal to
N^{3g-3+n+2|\mathcal{A}|}(=D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}})
.

(3)

For
\rho:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
, write
\mu(\rho):=(\rho(\alpha(p_{\partial_{1}})),\ldots,\rho(\alpha(p_{\partial_{|\mathcal{A}|}}))\in(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{|\mathcal{A}|}
.
Let
\widehat{\rho}=(\rho,h_{p_{i}})\in\widehat{\underline{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

(i)

If
\mu(\rho)\in(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0})^{|\mathcal{A}|}
and for each
1\leq i\leq k
, either
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))\neq\pm 2
or
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))=\pm 2
and
h_{p_{i}}=\mp 2
, then
\widehat{\rho}
belongs to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

(ii)

Suppose that either
\mu(\rho)\in(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1})^{|\mathcal{A}|}
or that
\mu(\rho)\in(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0})^{|\mathcal{A}|}
and there exists
i
such that
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))=\pm 2
and
h_{p_{i}}\neq\mp 2
, then
\widehat{\rho}
does not belong to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Remark 8.2
.

Soon after the prepublication of the present paper, Yu computed in
[
Yu23a
]
the center and PI-degrees of every stated skein algebras, thus generalizing the first and second items of Theorem
8.1
. As detailed in Subsection
8.4
, in the particular case where
|\mathcal{A}|=1
, our theorem re-proves some results of
[
BR22
,
BR21
,
GJS
]
.

8.2.
Quantum groups and their braided transmutation

Let us introduce two marked surfaces:
the
bigon

\mathbb{B}=(D^{2},\{a_{L},a_{r}\})
is a disc with two boundary edges; the
punctured monogon

\mathbf{m}_{1}=(\Sigma_{0,2},\{b\})
is an annulus with a single boundary edge. The skein algebras of the bigon and the punctured monogon have quantum group interpretations as follows.

Definition 8.3

(Quantum groups and their transmutation)
.

Set
q:=A^{2}
.

(1)

The
quantum group

\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is the algebra defined by the generators
a,b,c,d
and relations

ab=q^{-1}ba,\quad ac=q^{-1}ca,\quad db=qbd,\quad dc=qcd,\quad ad=1+q^{-1}bc,\quad da=1+qbc,\quad bc=cb.

(2)

The
braided quantum group

B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is the algebra with the same underlying vector space than
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
but with product given by

\displaystyle ba=q^{2}ab,\quad ca=q^{-2}ac,\quad da=ad,\quad bc=cb+(1-q^{-2})a(d-a)

\displaystyle db=bd+(1-q^{-2})ab,\quad cd=dc+(1-q^{-2})ca,\quad,ad-q^{2}cb=1.

(3)

The
Frobenius morphism

Fr:\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\to\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is the central embedding defined by
Fr(x):=x^{N}
for
x=a,b,c,d
.

Remark 8.4
.

The quantum group
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
has a natural coribbon Hopf algebra structure with coproduct and antipode

\begin{pmatrix}\Delta(a)&\Delta(b)\\
\Delta(c)&\Delta(d)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\boxtimes\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\quad\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon(a)&\epsilon(b)\\
\epsilon(c)&\epsilon(d)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\begin{pmatrix}S(a)&S(b)\\
S(c)&S(d)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d&-qb\\
-q^{-1}c&a\end{pmatrix}.

and with co-R matrix and co-twist given by

r\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\boxtimes\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}q^{1/2}&0&0&0\\
0&0&q^{-1/2}&0\\
0&q^{-1/2}&q^{1/2}-q^{-3/2}&0\\
0&0&0&q^{1/2}\end{pmatrix}\quad\Theta\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=-q^{3/2}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Majid’s transmutation is a procedure which associates to any coribbon Hopf
H
algebra an Hopf algebra object
BH
in the category
H-\operatorname{Comod}
where
BH=H
as a coalgebra and the
transmuted product
is given by

\underline{\mu}(x\otimes y):=\sum x_{(2)}y_{(2)}r(S(x_{(1)})x_{(3)}\otimes S(y_{(1)}))

and the
transmuted antipode
is

\underline{S}(x)=\sum S(x_{(2)})r((S^{2}(x_{(3)})S(x_{(1)})\otimes x_{(4)}))

B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is the transmutation of
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
so this explains its origin. We refer to
[
Maj90
,
Maj94
,
Maj95
]
for details.

For
i,j\in\{-,+\}
, consider the elements
\beta_{ij}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{ArcBigon.eps}}}}}}}$}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{B})
and
\gamma_{ij}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{ArcMonogon.eps}}}}}}}$}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{m}_{1})
.

Theorem 8.5
.

(1)

(
[
KQ19
,
CL22
]
) One has an algebra isomorphism
\varphi_{1}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{B})\cong\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
sending
\beta_{++}
,
\beta_{+-}
,
\beta_{-+}
,
\beta_{--}
to
a,b,c,d
respectively.

(2)

(
[
CL22
]
, see also
[
KM
,
LS
]
) One has an algebra isomorphism
\varphi_{2}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{m}_{1})\cong B_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
defined by

\varphi_{2}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-A^{5/2}\\
A^{1/2}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{++}&\gamma_{+-}\\
\gamma_{-+}&\gamma_{--}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-A^{5/2}\gamma_{-+}&-A^{5/2}\gamma_{--}\\
A^{1/2}\gamma_{++}&A^{1/2}\gamma_{+-}\end{pmatrix}.

Note that both isomorphisms
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
intertwines the Frobenius morphisms coming from quantum groups and skein algebras (here we consider that
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
are equal as
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
modules).

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
Z^{0}\subset\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
the image of the Frobenius morphism.

(1)

(
[
DCL94
]
, see also
[
BG02a
, Proposition
2.2
]
)
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is free over
Z^{0}
of rank
N^{3}
.

(2)

(
[
DRZ00
]
) An explicit basis of
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
over
Z^{0}
is given by

\mathcal{B}_{0}:=\{a^{m}b^{n}c^{s^{\prime}}:1\leq m\leq s^{\prime}\leq N-1,0\leq n\leq N-1\}\sqcup\{b^{n}c^{s^{\prime\prime}}d^{r}:0\leq n,r\leq N-1,0\leq s^{\prime\prime}\leq N-1-r\}.

Let
\gamma_{p}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{m}_{1})
be the peripheral curve around the unique inner puncture. A simple skein computation shows that
\gamma_{p}=A^{1/2}\gamma_{-+}-A^{5/2}\gamma_{+-}
so
\omega:=\varphi_{2}^{-1}(\gamma_{p})=-q^{-1}a-qd\in B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
Z^{\prime}\subset B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
be the subalgebra generated by
Z^{0}
and
\omega
. Then
\dim_{Q(Z^{\prime})}(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\otimes_{Z^{\prime}}Q(Z^{\prime}))=N
.

Proof.

As a module over
Z^{0}
,
Z^{\prime}\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$Z^{0}[\omega]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(T_{N}(\omega)=Fr(a+d))$}\right.}
so
Z^{\prime}
is freely generated over
Z^{0}
of rank
N
. Since
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is freely generated over
Z^{0}
of rank
N^{2}
, we obtain

\dim_{Q(Z^{\prime})}(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\otimes_{Z^{\prime}}Q(Z^{\prime}))=\frac{[B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}:Z^{0}]}{[Z^{\prime}:Z^{0}]}=\frac{N^{2}}{N}=N.

∎

For now on and by abuse of notations, we will identify
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
with
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{B})
and
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
with
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{m}_{1})
using
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
respectively.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a marked surface and
\alpha\subset\Sigma
an arc. Let
v,w
be the endpoints of
\alpha
and
a,b
be the boundary edges containing
v,w
respectively. If
a=b
we suppose that
v<_{a}w
. Write
\alpha_{ij}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
for the class of
\alpha
with state
s(v)=i
and
s(w)=j
. When
a\neq b
, one has an algebra morphism

\varphi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\quad\varphi_{\alpha}(\beta_{ij}):=\alpha_{ij}.

If
a=b
, one has an algebra morphism

\varphi_{\alpha}:B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\quad\varphi_{\alpha}(\gamma_{ij}):=\alpha_{ij}.

Note that, since
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
are equal as vector spaces, in both cases (
a=b
or
a\neq b
) we get a linear morphism
\varphi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Theorem 8.8
.

(
[
Kor23
, Corollary
3.8
]
)
Suppose that
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is essential and let
\Gamma
be a presenting graph with edges
\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
. Consider the linear morphism

\varphi^{\Gamma}:=\otimes_{\alpha\in\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)}\varphi_{\alpha}:(\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)}\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\quad\varphi^{\Gamma}(x_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{n})=\varphi_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\ldots\varphi_{\alpha_{n}}(x_{n}).

Then
\varphi^{\Gamma}
is a linear isomorphism which intertwines the Frobenius morphisms
Fr_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
and
Fr^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)}
.

Here we have identified
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\cong\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
with
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)}
using the isomorphism
\varphi_{\Gamma}
of Section
3
which sends a representation
\rho
to
(\rho(\alpha_{i}))_{i=1,\ldots,n}
. Note that
\varphi^{\Gamma}
is a quantum analogue of
\varphi_{\Gamma}
.

8.3.
Proof of Theorem
8.1

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be a connected essential marked surface with underlying surface
\Sigma_{g,n}
and let
\mathring{n}:=|\mathring{\mathcal{P}}|
represent the number of inner punctures. Let

\underline{Z}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\subset\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
be the algebra generated by the image of the Frobenius and the peripheral curves
\gamma_{p}
for
p\in\mathring{\mathcal{P}}
and denote by
\underline{Z}^{0}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\subset\underline{Z}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
the image of the Frobenius morphism.

(1)

As a
\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{0}
-module,
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is free of rank
R_{0}=N^{6g-6+3|\mathcal{A}|+3n}
.

(2)

Let
K:=Q(\underline{Z}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})
be the fraction field of
\underline{Z}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
. Then
R:=\dim_{K}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\otimes_{\underline{Z}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}K=R_{0}N^{-\mathring{n}}
.

(3)

Moreover if
\mathbf{\Sigma}
contains at most one boundary edge per boundary component, then
R=(D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}})^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma
be the presenting of Example
3.4
where
\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)
is obtained from
\mathbb{G}
by removing a corner arc
\alpha(p_{\partial_{0}})
.
By Theorem
8.8
, one has the isomorphism
\varphi^{\Gamma}:=\otimes_{\alpha\in\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)}\varphi_{\alpha}:(\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
which commutes with the Frobenius morphisms. By Theorem
8.6
,
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is free over
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
of rank
N^{3}
so
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\cong(\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)}
is free over
Z^{0}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\cong(\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}])^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)}
of rank

R_{0}:=[\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}):\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{0}]=[\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}:Z^{0}]^{|\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)|}=(N^{3})^{2g-2+n+|\mathcal{A}|}=N^{6g-6+3n+3|\mathcal{A}|}.

Note that an explicit basis for
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
over
Z^{0}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
is given by
\varphi^{\Gamma}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)})
.
Consider the decomposition
\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)=\mathcal{E}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{2}
where
\mathcal{E}_{1}
corresponds to the edges of the form
\gamma_{p}
with
p\in\mathcal{\mathring{P}}
. Then
\varphi^{\Gamma}
sends isomorphically the subspace
(Z^{\prime})^{\otimes\mathcal{E}_{1}}\otimes(Z^{0})^{\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2}}
to
\underline{Z}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
. So using Lemma
8.7
and writing
n=\mathring{n}+n^{\partial}
, we obtain the equalities

R:=\dim_{K}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=(\dim_{Q(Z)}B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\otimes_{Z})^{|\mathcal{E}_{1}|}([\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}:Z^{0}])^{|\mathcal{E}_{2}|}=(N^{2})^{\mathring{n}}(N^{3})^{2g-2+n_{\partial}+|\mathcal{A}|}=R_{0}N^{-\mathring{n}}.

Eventually, if
\mathbf{\Sigma}
has at most one boundary edge per boundary component, then
|\mathcal{A}|=n^{\partial}
so

R=N^{6g-6+2\mathring{n}+3n^{\partial}+3|\mathcal{A}|}=N^{6g-6+2(\mathring{n}+n^{\partial})+4|\mathcal{A}|}=\left(N^{3g-3+n+2|\mathcal{A}|}\right)^{2}=(D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}})^{2}.

∎

We now want to define the localization
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})^{loc}=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})[(\alpha(p_{\partial_{j}})_{-+})^{-1}]
obtained from
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
by localizing by all bad arcs
\alpha(p_{{\partial}_{j}})_{-+}
. Recall that if
R
is a ring and
S\subset R
is a multiplicative subset not containing
0
, then the localization
R[S^{-1}]
has a well-defined ring structure if
S
satisfies the following
Ore condition
:

\forall b,s\in R\times S,\quad\exists b_{1},s_{1}\in R\times S,\mbox{ such that }bs_{1}=sb_{1}.

In this case, we can endow
R[S^{-1}]
with the product
(as^{-1})\cdot(bt^{-1}):=(ab_{1})(ts_{1})^{-1}
.

Lemma 8.10
.

(
[
LYa
, Lemma
4.4(a)
]
) For every bad arc
\alpha_{bad}
and every basis element
[D,s]\in\mathcal{B}
there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\alpha_{bad}[D,s]=A^{n}[D,s]\alpha_{bad}
. In particular
the multiplicative subset
S\subset\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
generated by bad arcs satisfies the Ore condition.

Therefore
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})^{loc}=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})[(\alpha(p_{\partial_{j}})_{-+})^{-1}]
is well defined.

Lemma 8.11
.

j(\alpha(p_{\partial_{j}})_{-+})
is invertible in
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
so the morphism
j
induces a morphism

j^{loc}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})^{loc}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}).

Moreover

\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
is generated, as an algebra, by the image of
j^{loc}
together with its center
Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
. In particular
j\left(\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\right)\subset Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
.

Proof.

Let us first assume that
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{m}_{1}
. Then
\mathbf{m}_{1}^{*}=\mathbb{D}_{1}
, so
j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
embeds
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
into
D_{q}B
. This map is a skein analogue of a celebrated Majid’s morphism in quantum groups theory (see
[
Maj95
]
). Let
\alpha_{ij},\beta_{kl}\in D_{q}B
be the stated arcs defined in Section
4
(so
\alpha_{-+}=\beta_{+-}=0
). A simple computation, shown in Figure
10
, shows that

\displaystyle j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(\gamma_{++})=A^{-1/2}\beta_{++}\alpha_{+-},

\displaystyle j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(\gamma_{--})=A^{-1/2}\beta_{-+}\alpha_{--},

\displaystyle j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(\gamma_{-+})=A^{-1/2}\beta_{++}\alpha_{--},

\displaystyle j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(\gamma_{+-})=A^{-1/2}\beta_{-+}\alpha_{+-}-A^{-5/2}\beta_{--}\alpha_{++}.

Figure 10.
An illustration of the equality
j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(\gamma_{ij})=A^{-1/2}\beta_{j+}\alpha_{i-}-A^{-5/2}\beta_{j-}\alpha_{i+}
.

In particular for the bad arc
\gamma_{-+}
we see that
j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(\gamma_{-+})
is invertible with inverse
j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(\gamma_{-+})^{-1}=A^{1/2}\alpha_{++}\beta_{--}
. From these formulas, together with the fact that
\alpha_{++}\beta_{++},\alpha_{--}\beta_{--}\in Z_{\mathbb{D}_{1}}
and that the peripheral central element
\gamma_{p}
associated to the unique inner puncture
p
of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
satisfies

\gamma_{p}=-q^{-1}\beta_{--}\alpha_{++}-q\beta_{++}\alpha_{--}+\beta_{-+}\alpha_{+-}\in Z_{\mathbb{D}_{1}},

we deduce that the algebra
A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}\subset D_{q}B
generated by the image of
j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}^{loc}
together with
Z_{\mathbb{D}_{1}}
, contains every elements of the form
\beta_{kl}\alpha_{ij}
except possibly
\beta_{--}\alpha_{+-}
and
\beta_{-+}\alpha_{++}
. To prove that these two elements also belong to
A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
, note that
\beta_{--}^{2}=(\beta_{--}\alpha_{++})(\beta_{--}\alpha_{--})\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
so
\beta_{--}\alpha_{+-}=\beta_{--}^{2}(\beta_{++}\alpha_{+-})\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
. Similarly,
\beta_{-+}\alpha_{++}=(\beta_{-+}\alpha_{--})\alpha_{++}^{2}=(\beta_{-+}\alpha_{--})(\beta_{--}\alpha_{++})(\beta_{++}\alpha_{++})\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
.
Therefore for every
i,j,k,l\in\{\pm\}
,
\beta_{kl}\alpha_{ij}\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
. So
\alpha_{ij}^{2}=(\beta_{++}\alpha_{ij})(\beta_{--}\alpha_{ij})\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
and similarly
\beta_{kl}^{2}\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
.
Write
N=2n+1
and recall that
\alpha_{ij}^{N},\beta_{kl}^{N}\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
. So
\alpha_{++}=\left(\alpha_{++}^{2}\right)^{n+1}\alpha_{--}^{N}\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
and similarly
\alpha_{--},\beta_{++},\beta_{--}\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
.
This implies that
\alpha_{+-}=\beta_{++}(\beta_{--}\alpha_{+-})\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
and similarly
\beta_{-+}=(\beta_{-+}\alpha_{++})\alpha_{--}\in A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
. So all elements
\alpha_{ij},\beta_{kl}
belong to
A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}
. Since these elements generate
D_{q}B
, we have proved that
A_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}=D_{q}B
.

When
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma,\mathcal{A})
is a general surface satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
8.1
, for each boundary puncture
p_{\partial_{j}}
contained in a boundary component
\partial_{j}
, by embedding
\mathbf{m}_{1}
into a neighborhood of
\partial_{j}
, we obtain an algebra morphism
\mu_{q}^{j}:B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
sending
\alpha(p)_{kl}
to
\alpha(p_{\partial_{j}})_{kl}
. The morphism

\mu_{q}:=\otimes_{j=1}^{|\mathcal{A}|}\mu_{q}^{j}:(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\otimes\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})

is called the
quantum moment map
in literature (see
[
Kor22
,
Kor21a
]
and reference therein). Similarly, by embedding a copy of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
in the neighborhood of
\partial_{j}
seen as a boundary component of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
this time (recall that
\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
but that each
\partial_{j}
contains now two boundary edges), we obtain an algebra morphism
\mu_{q}^{*}:(D_{q}B)^{\otimes|\mathcal{A}|}\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\otimes|\mathcal{A}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D_{q}B)^{\otimes|\mathcal{A}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}})^{\otimes|\mathcal{A}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{q}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}

Figure 11.
An illustration of the quantum moment maps
\mu_{q}
and
\mu_{q}^{*}
.

So the fact that
j_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(\alpha(p)_{-+})
is invertible implies that
j_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\alpha(p_{\partial_{j}})_{-+})
is invertible as well so
j^{loc}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
is well defined. Let
A_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
be the subalgebra generated by the image of
j_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{loc}
and the center
Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
. By the preceding case and the commutativity of the above diagram, we see the image
\operatorname{Im}(\mu_{q}^{*})
of
\mu_{q}^{*}
is included in
A_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
. Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{|\mathcal{A}|}
be the boundary edges of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
. By construction, each
a_{i}
corresponds to two boundary edges
a_{i}^{\prime}
and
a_{i}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
and the image of
j_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
is spanned by the classes
[D,s]
of stated diagrams such that
D\cap a_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
for all
1\leq i\leq|\mathcal{A}|
. Let
[D,s]\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
be the class of an arbitrary stated diagram. As illustrated in Figure
12
, by performing skein relations in small neighborhoods of each boundary component
\partial_{j}
, we see that
[D,s]
is equal to a sum
[D,s]=\sum_{i}x_{i}[D_{i},s_{i}]
where
x_{i}\in\operatorname{Im}(\mu_{q}^{*})
and
D_{i}\cap a_{j}^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
for all
j
. This proves that
[D,s]\in A_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
so
A_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
and the proof is complete.

Figure 12.
Using a skein relation, we can write any class
[D,s]
as a linear combination of products of elements in the image of
\mu_{q}^{*}
(here corner arcs
\alpha(p)_{j+}
and
\alpha(p)_{j-}
) and elements
[D_{i},s_{i}]
where the
D_{i}
do not intersect the boundary arcs
a^{\prime\prime}
(here the boundary arc on the right).

∎

Lemma 8.12
.

We have
\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=\underline{Z}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.11
, we have
j(\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})=Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}\cap\operatorname{Im}(j)
so we need to prove the inclusion
Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}\cap\operatorname{Im}(j)\subset j(\underline{Z}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})
to conclude. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
be the Lê bases of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
respectively. So they are made of the classes
[D,s]
where
D
is simple,
s
is
\mathfrak{o}^{+}
increasing with the additional assumption that
[D,s]\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
does not contain any bad arc. Since
j
sends
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
to the subset
j(\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
of
[D,s]
which do not intersect the boundary edges
a^{\prime\prime}
for
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we see that
\operatorname{Im}(j)
is spanned by
j(\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})
.
For
(D,s)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
, denote by
T(D,s)
the class of the diagram obtained from
(D,s)
as follows. Fix an arbitrary total order
\prec
on the set of connected diagrams in
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
. Write
(D,s)=\sqcup_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}^{(n_{i})}
for the connected components of
(D,s)
such that the
\beta_{i}
are pairwise distinct ordered by
i<j\Rightarrow\beta_{i}\prec\beta_{j}
and
\beta_{i}^{(n_{i})}
means that we have
n_{i}
parallel copies of
\beta_{i}
. If
\beta_{i}
is a loop, write
T(\beta_{i}^{(n_{i})}):=T_{n_{i}}(\beta_{i})\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
. If
\beta_{i}
is a stated arc, set
T(\beta_{i}^{(n_{i})})
to be the tangle made of
n_{i}
parallel copies of
\beta_{i}
pushed in the framing direction. Eventually set
T(D,s):=T(\beta_{1}^{(n_{1})})\ldots T(\beta_{m}^{(n_{m})})
(ordered product).
By
[
KQ19
, Proposition
2.14
]
the set
T\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
made of elements
T(D,s)
for
[D,s]\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
is a basis of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
. On the one hand,
Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
is spanned by the subset
S_{1}\subset T\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
made of elements
T(D,s)
where in the decomposition
(D,s)=\sqcup_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}^{(n_{i})}
for every
1\leq i\leq n
then either
\beta_{i}
is a peripheral curve
\gamma_{p}
around an inner puncture, a boundary element
\alpha_{\partial}^{\pm 1}
or
N
divides
n_{i}
. On the other hand,
\operatorname{Im}(j)
is spanned by the subset
S_{2}\subset T\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
made of elements
T(D,s)
such that
D
does not intersect any boundary edge
a^{\prime\prime}
for
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Therefore
j(\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})=Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}\cap\operatorname{Im}(j)
is spanned by
S_{1}\cap S_{2}
: these elements are all in
j(\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}^{\prime})
. So
j(\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}})=j(\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})
and we conclude using the injectivity of
j
.

∎

Let
\underline{\widehat{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\operatorname{MaxSpec}(\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}})
and
j^{*}:\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})\to\underline{\widehat{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
the dominant map defined by the embedding
j:\underline{Z}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\hookrightarrow Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
.

Lemma 8.13
.

The image of
j^{*}
is the set of elements
\widehat{\rho}=(\rho,h_{p_{1}},\ldots,h_{p_{k}})\in\underline{\widehat{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
such that
\mu(\rho)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}\times\ldots\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}
.

Proof.

For each boundary edge
a\in\mathcal{A}
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
fix a point
v_{a}\in a
and let
\mathbb{V}:=\{v_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
. By definition
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
is the marked surface with
\Sigma
as underlying surface and for which each boundary edge
a
has been replaced by two boundary edges
a^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\prime}
.
For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
fix
v_{a^{\prime}}:=v_{a}\in a^{\prime}
and
v_{a^{\prime\prime}}\in a^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathbb{V}^{*}=\{v_{a^{\prime}},v_{a^{\prime\prime}}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
. Using Theorem
3.2
, we identify
\underline{\widehat{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
with the set of elements
\widehat{\rho}=(\rho,h_{p_{i}})_{i}
where
\rho:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
h_{p_{i}}\in\mathbb{C}
is such that
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))=-T_{N}(h_{p_{i}})
. Recall that
\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
is identified as the set of elements
\widehat{\rho}^{*}=(\rho,h_{p_{i}},h_{\partial_{j}})_{i,j}
where
\rho:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V}^{*})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
sends every corner path
\alpha(p)
to an element of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
. Since
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{V}^{*}
, we have a full embedding
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\to\Pi_{1}(\Sigma^{*},\mathbb{V}^{*})
and thus a restriction morphism
\operatorname{Hom}(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma^{*},\mathbb{V}^{*}),\operatorname{SL}_{2})\to\operatorname{Hom}(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V}),\operatorname{SL}_{2})
sending
\rho^{*}
to
\mathchoice{{\rho^{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.9087pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\rho^{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.5087pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\rho^{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.27534pt,depth=2.41304pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\rho^{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.01665pt,depth=2.99861pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}
. By definition, the map
j^{*}
satisfies

j^{*}((\rho^{*},h_{p_{i}},h_{\partial_{j}}))=(\mathchoice{{\rho^{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.9087pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\rho^{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.5087pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\rho^{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=4.27534pt,depth=2.41304pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\rho^{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.01665pt,depth=2.99861pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}},h_{p_{i}}).

So given a functor
\rho:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
such that
\rho(\alpha(p))\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}
for all boundary puncture
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
, we need to prove that there exists a functor
\rho^{*}:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma^{*},\mathbb{V}^{*})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
such that
\mathchoice{{\phi_{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\phi_{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\phi_{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.88889pt,depth=2.41304pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}{{\phi_{*}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=2.99861pt}}_{\,\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})}}=\rho
and such that
\rho^{*}(\alpha(p^{\prime}))\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
for all boundary puncture
p^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
. For a boundary component
\partial
of
\Sigma
containing a boundary puncture
p_{\partial}\in P^{\partial}
, the same boundary component considered in
\Sigma^{*}
contains two boundary punctures
p_{\partial}^{\prime}
and
p_{\partial}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\alpha(p_{\partial})=\alpha(p^{\prime\prime}_{\partial})\alpha(p^{\prime}_{\partial})
. So lifting
\rho
to a functor
\rho^{*}
amounts to choose for each
p_{\partial}\in P^{\partial}
two elements
\rho^{*}(\alpha(p^{\prime}_{\partial})),\rho^{*}(\alpha(p^{\prime\prime}_{\partial}))\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
such that
\rho(\alpha(p_{\partial}))=\rho^{*}(\alpha(p^{\prime}_{\partial}))\rho^{*}(\alpha(p^{\prime\prime}_{\partial}))
. The existence of such lift thus follows from the surjectivity of the map

\pi:\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0},\quad\pi(A,B):=AB.

Indeed, denote by
B^{+},B^{-}\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}
the subalgebras of upper and lower triangular matrices and write
w:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
so that
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}=B^{-}B^{+}
and
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}=B^{-}wB^{+}
. Then given
M\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}
such that
M=M_{-}M_{+}
with
M_{\pm}\in B^{\pm}
set
A:=M_{-}w\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
and
B:=-wM_{+}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
then
\pi(AB)=M
. Thus
\pi
is surjective. This concludes the proof.

∎

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

Write
D:=D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
.
Let
\widehat{\rho}=(\rho,h_{p_{1}},\ldots,h_{p_{k}})\in\underline{\widehat{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
such that
\mu(\rho)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}\times\ldots\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}
and
\widehat{\rho}^{*}\in\widehat{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
such that
j^{*}(\widehat{\rho}^{*})=\widehat{\rho}
.
By Lemma
8.11
,
j
induces a surjective map

j_{\widehat{\rho}}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})_{\widehat{\rho}}\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})_{\widehat{\rho}^{*}}.

Let
r:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
be an irreducible representation with classical shadow
\widehat{\rho}^{*}
and
r^{\prime}:=r\circ j:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
. Then
r^{\prime}
has classical shadow
\widehat{\rho}
and is irreducible by surjectivity of
j_{\widehat{\rho}}
and the Schür lemma. First suppose that for all
i
, either
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))\neq\pm 2
or
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))=\pm 2
and
h_{p_{i}}=\mp 2
. By Theorem
7.2
,
\widehat{\rho}^{*}
belongs to the Azumaya locus of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
so
\dim(V)=D
. Since the locus
\mathcal{O}\subset\widehat{\underline{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
of such
\widehat{\rho}
is open dense, some of these irreducible representations
r^{\prime}
must have their classical shadows in the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. Therefore the PI-degree of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is
D
. This in turn implies that
\mathcal{O}
is included into the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Next suppose that there exists
i
such that
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))=\pm 2
and
h_{p_{i}}\neq\mp 2
. Then
\widehat{\rho}^{*}
does not belong to the Azumaya locus of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
so
\dim(V)<D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
. This implies that
\widehat{\rho}
does not belong to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Eventually consider
\widehat{\rho}\in\underline{\widehat{X}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
such that
\mu(\rho)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}\times\ldots\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
. We can consider an irreducible representation
r:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
such that the composition
r^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\xrightarrow{r}\operatorname{End}(V)
has classical shadow
\widehat{\rho}
. Since
D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}<D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
then
\dim(V)<D_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}
so
\widehat{\rho}
does not belong to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. This concludes the proof.

∎

8.4.
Quantum moduli algebras and lattice gauge field theory

In the particular case where
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{\Sigma}(g,n):=(\Sigma_{g,n+1},\{a\})
is a genus
g\geq 0
surface with
n+1
boundary components and exactly one of them has a single boundary edge
a
whereas the others are inner punctures, the algebra
\mathcal{L}_{g,n}:=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is called the
unrestricted quantum moduli algebra
. The algebra
\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
appeared in literature in various contexts.

(1)

They were first defined independently in
[
AGS95
,
AGS96
,
AS96
]
and
[
BR95
,
BR96
]
where they appeared as deformation quantization of the Fock-Rosly
[
FR99
]
and Alekseev-Kosmann-Malkin-Meinrenken
[
AM94
,
AKSM02
,
AM95
]
moduli spaces (see also
[
Kor19b
]
). Under this approach, they were intensively studied in
[
BR22
,
BR21
,
BFR23a
]
.

(2)

They were rediscovered independently by Habiro under the name
quantum representation variety
in
[
Hab12
]
.

(3)

As we just defined, they are particular cases of stated skein algebras.

(4)

They eventually appeared under the name
internal skein algebras
in the work of Ben Zvi-Brochier-Jordan
[
BZBJ18a
,
BZBJ18b
]
and further studied in
[
Coo23
,
GJS23
,
GJS
]
.

The equivalence between unrestricted quantum moduli algebrasand stated skein algebras is proved in
[
BFKB98
,
Fai20
,
Kor23
]
. The equivalence between internal skein algebras and unrestricted quantum moduli algebras is proved in
[
BZBJ18a
]
. The equivalence between internal skein algebras and stated skein algebras is proved in
[
Hai22
]
. The equivalence between quantum representation varieties and stated skein algebras is proved in
[
KM
]
.

By definition
\mathbf{\Sigma}(g,n)^{*}=(\Sigma_{g,n+1},\{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\})
is the surface
\Sigma_{g,n+1}
with two boundary edges
\{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\}
in the same boundary component. Note that
\mathbf{\Sigma}(g,n)^{*}
is obtained from
g
copies of
\mathbf{\Sigma}(1,0)^{*}
and
n
copies of
\mathbf{\Sigma}(0,1)^{*}
by gluing some pairs of boundary edges. Therefore one has a splitting morphism

\theta:\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(g,n)^{*})\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(1,0)^{*})^{\otimes g}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(0,1)^{*})^{\otimes n}.

Note that
\mathbf{\Sigma}(0,1)^{*}=\mathbb{D}_{1}
so
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(0,1)^{*})\cong D_{q}B
. On the other hand,
\mathcal{HH}_{q}:=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(1,0)^{*})
is the so-called
Heisenberg double
algebra. To see this, let
\alpha,\beta
be the arcs of Figure
13
and consider the matrices

N(\alpha):=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{++}&\alpha_{+-}\\
\alpha_{-+}&\alpha_{--}\end{pmatrix},N(\beta):=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{++}&\beta_{+-}\\
\beta_{-+}&\beta_{--}\end{pmatrix},\mathscr{R}:=\begin{pmatrix}A&0&0&0\\
0&0&A^{-1}&0\\
0&A^{-1}&A-A^{-3}&0\\
0&0&0&A\end{pmatrix}.

By
[
Kor23
, Theorem
1.1
]
,
\mathcal{HH}_{q}
is generated by the elements
\alpha_{ij},\beta_{kl}
with relations
\det_{q}(N(\alpha))=\det_{q}(N(\beta))=1
(where
\det_{q}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=ad-q^{-1}bc
) and

N(\alpha)\odot N(\alpha)=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(N(\alpha)\odot N(\alpha))\mathscr{R},N(\beta)\odot N(\beta)=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(N(\beta)\odot N(\beta))\mathscr{R},N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta)=\mathscr{R}(N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta))\mathscr{R}.

Here
\odot
denotes the Kronecker product and we recognize here Alekseev’s relations defining the Heisenberg double. Therefore
\overline{\mathcal{HH}}_{q}:=\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(1,0)^{*})
is the quotient of
\mathcal{HH}_{q}
by the ideal generated by the two bad arcs.

Figure 13.
The marked surface
\mathbf{\Sigma}(1,0)^{*}=(\Sigma_{1,1},\{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\})
defining the Heisenberg double
\mathcal{HH}_{q}
.

Consider the composition:

\Phi:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\xrightarrow{j}\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}(g,n)^{*})\xrightarrow{\theta}(\overline{\mathcal{HH}}_{q})^{\otimes g}\otimes(D_{q}B)^{\otimes n}.

An analogue of the map
\Phi
appeared in Alekseev’s work
[
Ale94
]
and was named
Alekseev’s morphism
in
[
BFR23a
]
. Given
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
and
W_{1},\ldots,W_{g}
some semi-weight indecomposable representations of
U_{q}\mathfrak{gl}_{2}
and
\overline{\mathcal{HH}}_{q}
respectively, using the morphism
\Phi
, we endow
W_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes W_{g}\otimes V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}
with a structure of module over
\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
.
Theorem
7.2
implies that every reduced
\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
module is isomorphic to a direct sum of such modules and proves that the representations
W_{i}
are in
1:1
correspondence with the character over the center of
\overline{\mathcal{HH}}_{q}
and gives a complete classification for the modules
V_{i}
. Therefore we obtain an explicit construction of the reduced representations of the unrestricted quantum moduli algebras.
The map
\mu:\underline{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
sending
\rho
to
\rho(\alpha_{\partial})
was named the
moment map
in
[
AKSM02
]
.
Theorem
8.1
implies

Corollary 8.14
.

(1)

The center of
\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is generated by the image of the Frobenius and the peripheral curves
\gamma_{p_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{p_{n}}
.

(2)

The PI-degree of
\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is
N^{3g+n}
.

(3)

The Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
is the locus of elements
\widehat{\rho}=(\rho,h_{p_{i}})
such that
\mu(\rho)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}
and
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p_{i}}))=\pm 2\Rightarrow h_{p_{i}}=\mp 2
.

When
n=0
, Corollary
8.14
was proved in
[
GJS
, Theorem
1
]
. When
g=0
, the first point of Corollary
8.14
was proved in
[
BR22
, Theorem
1.1
]
, while the second point was proved in
[
BR21
, Theorem
1.3
]
.

Let us end by a discussion on the classification of representations of
\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
. Let
r:\mathcal{L}_{g,n}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
be an indecomposable semi-weight representation and consider the only bad arc
\alpha(p)_{-+}\in\mathcal{L}_{g,n}
.

(1)

If
r(\alpha(p)_{-+})
is invertible, then
r
is isomorphic to a reduced representation, i.e. it factorizes through
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
. These representations are completely classified by Theorem
7.2
and the skein Alekseev morphism
\Phi
permits an explicit construction of them as explained before. Note that every irreducible representation whose classical shadow belongs to the Azumaya locus belongs to this class.

(2)

If
r(\alpha(p)_{-+})=0
,
r
factorizes through the reduced stated skein algebra
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. When
n=0
, by
[
Kor22
]
the latter algebra is Azumaya so these representations are classified. When
n\geq 1
, the techniques of the present paper are insufficient to classify such representations though we conjecture that Theorem
7.2
still holds in this case.

(3)

The last class of representations, for which
r(\alpha(p)_{-+})^{N}=0
but
r(\alpha(p)_{-+})\neq 0
, seems very difficult to classify. Such a classification might even be an unsolvable problem.

Appendix A
Glossary

We summarize the notations and definitions used in the paper.

Marked surfaces

\bullet
A
marked surface
is a pair
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma,\mathcal{A})
where
\Sigma
is a compact oriented surface and
\mathcal{A}\subset\partial\Sigma
a finite collection of pairwise disjoint arcs named
boundary edges
.
A connected component of
\partial\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{A}
is a
puncture
; it is an
inner puncture
if it is a circle, else it is a
boundary puncture
. We decompose as
\mathcal{P}=\mathring{\mathcal{P}}\sqcup\mathcal{P}^{\partial}
the set of punctures. We denote by
\Gamma^{\partial}\subset\pi_{0}(\partial\Sigma)
the subset of boundary components which are not inner punctures.

\bullet

\mathbf{\Sigma}
is
essential
if each connected component of
\Sigma
contains some boundary edge.
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is a
2P-marked surface
if it has no inner puncture and each connected component of
\Sigma
contains at least two boundary edges.

\bullet
